Linear vortex symmetrization: the spectral density function Thanks: The first author was supported in part by NSF grant DMS-2007008. The second author was supported in part by NSF grant DMS-1945179.
Alexandru D. Ionescu, Hao Jia

Abstract
. We investigate solutions of the 2 ​ d 2d incompressible Euler equations, linearized around steady states which are radially decreasing vortices. Our main goal is to understand the smoothness of what we call the spectral density function associated with the linearized operator, which we hope will be a step towards proving full nonlinear asymptotic stability of radially decreasing vortices. The motivation for considering the spectral density function is that it is not possible to describe the vorticity or the stream function in terms of one modulated profile. There are in fact two profiles, both at the level of the physical vorticity and at the level of the stream function. The spectral density function allows us to identify these profiles, and its smoothness leads to pointwise decay of the stream function which is consistent with the decay estimates first proved in Bedrossian-Coti Zelati-Vicol in [ 5 ] .



1.
Introduction and main results

In this paper we study the two dimensional incompressible Euler equations on the plane, in the vorticity formulation. Consider solutions
\omega(t,x,y):[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\partial_{t}\omega+u\cdot\nabla\omega=0,\quad u=\nabla^{\perp}\psi,\quad\Delta\psi=\omega,

(1.1)

for
(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty)
.
The two dimensional incompressible Euler equation is globally well-posed for smooth initial data, by the classical result of Wolibner
[
53
]
. (See also
[
49
,
50
]
for global well-posedness results with rough initial data, such as
L^{\infty}
vorticity.) The long time behavior of general solutions is however very difficult to understand, due to the lack of a global relaxation mechanism.

There have been some attempts in building a theory of “weak turbulence” for the two dimensional Euler equation, to explain the appearance of coherent structures, see e.g., chapter 34 of
[
47
]
. A proposed mathematical explanation is that, generically, the vorticity
\omega(t)
converges
weakly
but not strongly as
t\to\infty
. A rigorous proof of such a conjecture is way beyond the reach of current PDE techniques. A more realistic approach is to consider the 2D Euler equations in physically relevant perturbative regimes, such as around shear flows and vortices. In this paper we study the case of vortices.

The presence of coherent vortices is a prominent feature in two dimensional fluid flows, such as viscous flows with high Reynolds number and perfect fluid flows. These vortices are believed to play an important role in the 2D turbulence theory (see for example
[
2
,
3
,
6
,
7
,
37
,
38
]
). Numerical and physical experiments and formal asymptotic analysis (see
[
2
,
3
,
45
]
and references therein) suggest that small perturbations of vortices form spirals around the center of the vortex and the angle-dependent modes of the vorticity vanish in the weak sense as
t\to\infty
, which leads to “axi-symmetrization” of the vorticity.

The stability analysis of fluid flows is among the oldest problems studied in hydrodynamics, starting with Kelvin
[
26
]
, Orr
[
42
]
, Rayleigh
[
43
]
, and continuing to the present day, see for example
[
17
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
45
,
51
,
54
,
55
]
and references therein. Arnold
[
1
]
proved an important criteria for nonlinear stability of some steady states using monotonicity formulas, but the precise dynamics near these solutions are not known. We also refer to
[
11
]
for recent results on the nonlinear stability of vortices in this direction. The vortex symmetrization phenomenon has been studied rigorously at the linearized level around a strictly decreasing vortex profile by Bedrossian–Coti Zelati–Vicol
[
5
]
, who established axi-symmetrization of the vorticity and optimal rates of decay of the associated stream function.

1.1.
Motivation and the spectral density function

Extending the linearized stability analysis for inviscid fluid equations to the full nonlinear setting is a challenging problem. In the case of monotonic shear flows on the bounded channel
\mathbb{T}\times[0,1]
the nonlinear stability problem has been recently solved by the authors
[
21
]
, and independently by Masmoudi-Zhao in
[
36
]
, following earlier important work of Bedrossian-Masmoudi
[
4
]
, and the authors
[
22
]
on the nonlinear stability of the Couette flow.

In
[
23
]
we proved nonlinear asymptotic stability near point vortices in the plane, which appears to be the only nonlinear asymptotic stability result for the two dimensional Euler equation in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The case of general vortices, though, presents several new difficulties. To explain these new features, let us recall the main perturbation equations.

We work with the polar coordinate
(r,\theta)
. The velocity field
U(r)e_{\theta}
gives a steady state for
2d
Euler equations, for any radial function
U(r)
(with mild assumptions on regularity and decay at
\infty
). Let
\Omega(r)
be the associated vorticity. Then from Biot-Savart law,
U(r)
satisfies

\partial_{r}U(r)+U(r)/r=\Omega(r),\,\,r\in(0,\infty),\quad{\rm with}\,\,\lim_{r\to 0}U(r)=\lim_{r\to\infty}U(r)=0.

(1.2)

The linearized two dimensional Euler equation around
U(r)e_{\theta}
is

\partial_{t}\omega+\frac{U(r)}{r}\partial_{\theta}\omega-\frac{\Omega^{\prime}(r)}{r}\partial_{\theta}\psi=0,\qquad(\theta,r)\in\mathbb{T}\times(0,\infty),

(1.3)

with initial data
\omega_{0}(\theta,r)
. The stream function
\psi
is determined through

\left(\partial_{rr}+r^{-1}\partial_{r}+r^{-2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}\right)\psi=\omega,\qquad(\theta,r)\in\mathbb{T}\times(0,\infty).

(1.4)

We assume the orthogonality conditions

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{0}(\theta,r)(\cos{\theta},\sin{\theta})\,r^{2}drd\theta=0,\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\int_{\mathbb{T}}\omega_{0}(\theta,r)\,d\theta=0.

(1.5)

The general case can be reduced to the case with the orthogonality conditions (
1.5
) by re-centering a slightly modified
\Omega(r)
.

Taking Fourier transform in
\theta
in the equation (
1.3
)-(
1.4
) for
\omega
, we obtain that

\partial_{t}\omega_{k}+ik\frac{U(r)}{r}\omega_{k}-ik\frac{\Omega^{\prime}(r)}{r}\psi_{k}=0,\quad(\partial_{r}^{2}+r^{-1}\partial_{r}-k^{2}r^{-2})\psi_{k}=\omega_{k},

(1.6)

for
k\in\mathbb{Z},t\geq 0,r\in(0,\infty)
. Here
\omega_{k}
and
\psi_{k}
are the
k-
th Fourier coefficients of
\omega
and
\psi
.

The equations (
1.3
)-(
1.4
) may be compared with the linearized equation around shear flows in a finite channel, where we have for
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
,

\partial_{t}\omega_{k}+ikb(y)\omega-ikb^{\prime\prime}(y)\psi_{k}=0,\quad(\partial_{y}^{2}-k^{2})\psi_{k}=\omega_{k},\quad\psi_{k}(t,0)=\psi_{k}(t,1)=0,

(1.7)

for
(t,x,y)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}\times[0,1]
. Under suitable assumptions on the function
b
and assuming that
\omega|_{t=0}
is smooth (or Gevrey smooth) and compactly supported in
\mathbb{T}\times(0,1)
, one can prove that the solution
\omega_{k}(t,y)
can be represented essentially in the form

\omega_{k}(t,y)=f_{k}(t,y)e^{-ikb(y)t},

(1.8)

for
y\in[0,1],t\in[0,\infty)
. The point is that the
profile

f_{k}(t,y)
is smooth (or Gevrey smooth) in
y
, uniformly over
t\in[0,\infty)
(and stays compactly supported in
(0,1)
as well). Such formulas and quantitative bounds on
f
are very important for passing to the nonlinear problem, since the main point of the nonlinear analysis is to identify suitable analogous nonlinear profiles and establish their (sliding) smoothness uniformly in time. See
[
25
]
and the recent papers
[
21
,
36
]
.

In our case, the situation is different. The main issue that the linearized flow corresponding to (
1.6
) has no “uniformly smooth profile” even after taking off oscillatory factors. Instead, for smooth (or Gevrey smooth) initial data, we should decompose for
r>0
,

\omega_{k}(t,r)=f_{k1}(t,r)e^{-ik(U(r)/r)t}+f_{k2}(t,r),

(1.9)

where
f_{k1}
and
f_{k2}
are both uniformly smooth in
r
over
t\in(0,\infty)
. See Theorem
1.5
for the details. This decomposition has natural physical meaning, since the first term comes from the interior of the fluid itself, while the second term is generated by the “boundary” corresponding to
r=0
(in the case of shear flows, there is no boundary contribution in (
1.8
) due to the support assumption on
\omega(t)
and
b^{\prime\prime}
). This structure presents a new and possibly significant difficulty for the nonlinear problem, since the method in the shear flow case relies crucially on obtaining control in smooth norms on a well defined profile for the vorticity.

At a more technical level, the presence of the term
ikU(r)/r
, which generates the oscillatory part of the vorticity, has a degeneracy when
r
approaches
r=0
, that is,
\partial_{r}(U(r)/r)
approaches
0
as
r\to 0+
. This degeneracy coupled with the nonlocal term
ik(\Omega^{\prime}/r)\psi_{k}
in (
1.6
) leads to a new dynamical phenomenon, namely the
depletion of vorticity
from the origin, which refers to the fact that the vorticity enjoys better than expected decay near the critical point of the background flow. The vorticity depletion phenomenon was first observed by Bouchet-Morita
[
8
]
and proved by Wei–Zhang–Zhao
[
52
]
for shear flows with critical points. In the context of vortices, the vortex depletion phenomenon was proved by Bedrossian–Coti Zelati–Vicol
[
5
]
. The phenomenon is important in our proof here as well.

In this paper, we take a first step towards understanding the nonlinear vortex symmetrization problem, and prove linear vortex symmetrization in Gevrey spaces. See Theorems
1.4
-
1.5
below for the main results. More importantly, we propose a different strategy: instead of studying the profile for the vorticity function which is difficult to even define in view of (
1.9
), we focus on what we call
the spectral density function
. The spectral density function (which plays the role of a profile) is naturally associated with the linearized equation, and in our setting can be used to track both the vanishing of the various quantities in
r
(including the vorticity depletion phenomenon), and optimal regularity. Once we have the bounds on this spectral density functions, the estimates on the stream functions, velocity fields and the vorticity can be obtained by simple calculations.

The degeneracy at
r=0
makes it difficult to use the global change of coordinate
V=U(r)/r
, which is the natural analogue of what was used in the case of shear flows to be able to accurately define “resonant times”. In our problem it is simpler to work with the variable
v:=\log r
for
r\in(0,\infty)
. We also work here with Gevrey smoothness instead of Sobolev smoothness, as this is the expected framework of the nonlinear problem.

The use of spectral density functions resolves several conceptual difficulties in the study of the nonlinear axi-symmetrization problem, since we can prove optimal regularity bounds on them, and they capture both physical space decay and regularity. However, it remains open how to define the correct spectral density function in the nonlinear setting, and to derive the right evolution equations and prove bounds. We hope to address these issues in the future.

1.2.
Main equations and assumptions on the background flow

We assume that the background radial vorticity profile
\Omega(r)
satisfy the following natural conditions.

Assumption 1.1
.

There exist constants
C_{\ast}\in(0,\infty)
and
c_{\ast}\in(-\infty,0)
such that for all
r\in(0,\infty)
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,\infty)
we have

\begin{split}&0<\Omega(r)\leq\frac{C_{\ast}}{\langle r\rangle^{6}},\quad\partial_{r}\Omega(r)<0,\quad\Big|(r\partial_{r})^{j}\Big(\frac{\Omega^{\prime}(r)}{r}-\frac{c_{\ast}}{\langle r\rangle^{8}}\Big)\Big|\leq\frac{C_{\ast}^{j+1}(j!)^{2}}{\langle r\rangle^{10}}r^{2}.\end{split}

(1.10)

In the above, we used the notation
\langle x\rangle:=\sqrt{x^{2}+2}
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Define for
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}:=(0,\infty)
,

b(r):=U(r)/r,\qquad d(r)=\Omega^{\prime}(r)/r.

(1.11)

It follows from (
1.10
)-(
1.11
) that for
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

b(r)=U(r)/r=\int_{0}^{1}s\,\Omega(rs)\,ds\approx\frac{1}{\langle r\rangle^{2}},\qquad b^{\prime}(r)\approx-\frac{r}{\langle r\rangle^{4}},\qquad|b^{\prime\prime}(r)|\lesssim\frac{1}{\langle r\rangle^{4}}.

(1.12)

In the above the implied constants depend only on the constants
c_{\ast}
and
C_{\ast}
in (
1.10
).

Define for
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\},r,\rho\in(0,\infty)
, the function

G_{k}(r,\rho):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\rho}{2|k|}\Big(\frac{r}{\rho}\Big)^{|k|}&\qquad{\rm for}\,\,r<\rho,\\
\frac{\rho}{2|k|}\Big(\frac{\rho}{r}\Big)^{|k|}&\qquad{\rm for}\,\,r\geq\rho.\end{array}\right.

(1.13)

G_{k}(r,\rho)
is the Green’s function for the differential operator
-\partial_{r}^{2}-r^{-1}\partial_{r}+k^{2}r^{-2}
on
(0,\infty)
with vanishing boundary conditions.

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
, we set for any
f\in L^{2}\big(\mathbb{R}^{+},r^{2}|\Omega^{\prime}(r)|^{-1}dr\big)
,

L_{k}f(r)=\frac{U(r)}{r}f(r)+\frac{\Omega^{\prime}(r)}{r}\int_{0}^{\infty}G_{k}(r,\rho)f(\rho)\,d\rho,

(1.14)

The equation (
1.6
) can be reformulated as

\partial_{t}\omega_{k}+ikL_{k}\omega_{k}=0.

(1.15)

Define the space

X_{k}:=L^{2}\Big(\mathbb{R}^{+},\frac{r^{2}}{|\Omega^{\prime}(r)|}\,dr\Big),

(1.16)

with the natural norm that for any
g\in X_{k}
,

\|g\|_{X_{k}}^{2}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}|g(r)|^{2}\frac{r^{2}}{|\Omega^{\prime}(r)|}\,dr.

(1.17)

It is clear that
L_{k}:X_{k}\to X_{k}
is bounded and self adjoint. Since
L_{k}
is a compact perturbation of the simple multiplication operator
f\to b(r)f
, by general spectral theory, we can conclude that the spectrum of
L_{k}
consists of the continuous spectrum
[0,b(0)]
and possibly some discrete eigenvalues in
\mathbb{R}
. Our main assumptions (
1.10
)-(
1.29
) imply that there are no discrete eigenvalues, see section
4
for a simple proof.

The space
X_{k}
imposes strong conditions on the decay of functions inside
X_{k}
if
\Omega^{\prime}
decays fast in
r
. For our purposes, we can work with initial data with milder decay properties, see the assumption
1.3
, since we only use the space
X_{k}
in a qualitative way, see e.g. (
1.18
), and our bounds are quantitative, by a standard limiting argument.

By standard theory of spectral projections, we then have

\begin{split}&\omega_{k}(t,r)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ik\lambda t}\Big\{\left[(\lambda-i\epsilon-L_{k})^{-1}-(\lambda+i\epsilon-L_{k})^{-1}\right]\omega^{k}_{0}\Big\}(r)\,d\lambda\\
&=\frac{-1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-ikb(r_{0})t}b^{\prime}(r_{0})\Big\{\big[(-b(r_{0})-i\epsilon+L_{k})^{-1}-(-b(r_{0})+i\epsilon+L_{k})^{-1}\big]\omega^{k}_{0}\Big\}(r)\,dr_{0}.\end{split}

(1.18)

We then obtain from (
1.6
) that

\begin{split}\psi_{k}(t,r)&=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-ikb(r_{0})t}b^{\prime}(r_{0})\int_{0}^{\infty}G_{k}(r,\rho)\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\bigg\{\Big[(-b(r_{0})-i\epsilon+L_{k})^{-1}-(-b(r_{0})+i\epsilon+L_{k})^{-1}\Big]\omega^{k}_{0}\bigg\}(\rho)\,d\rho\,dr_{0}\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-ikb(r_{0})t}b^{\prime}(r_{0})\left[\psi_{k,\epsilon}^{-}(r,r_{0})-\psi_{k,\epsilon}^{+}(r,r_{0})\right]dr_{0}.\end{split}

(1.19)

In the above,

\begin{split}&\psi_{k,\epsilon}^{+}(r,r_{0}):=\int_{0}^{\infty}G_{k}(r,\rho)\Big[(-b(r_{0})+i\epsilon+L_{k})^{-1}\omega^{k}_{0}\Big](\rho)\,d\rho,\\
&\psi_{k,\epsilon}^{-}(r,r_{0}):=\int_{0}^{\infty}G_{k}(r,\rho)\Big[(-b(r_{0})-i\epsilon+L_{k})^{-1}\omega^{k}_{0}\Big](\rho)\,d\rho.\end{split}

(1.20)

We note that
\psi_{k,\epsilon}^{+}(r,r_{0}),\psi_{k,\epsilon}^{-}(r,r_{0})
satisfy for
\iota\in\{+,-\}
and
r,r_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

\Big[k^{2}/r^{2}-r^{-1}\partial_{r}-\partial_{r}^{2}\Big]\psi_{k,\epsilon}^{\iota}(r,r_{0})+\frac{d(r)}{b(r)-b(r_{0})+i\iota\epsilon}\psi_{k,\epsilon}^{\iota}(r,r_{0})=\frac{\omega^{k}_{0}(r)}{b(r)-b(r_{0})+i\iota\epsilon}.

(1.21)

It is more convenient to work in the variable
v
with
r=e^{v}
for
r\in(0,\infty)
. We therefore introduce for
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\},\epsilon\in[-1/4,1/4]\backslash\{0\}
and
\iota\in\{+,-\}
,

\Pi^{\iota}_{k,\epsilon}(v,w):=\psi_{k,\epsilon}^{\iota}(r,r_{0}),\qquad{\rm where}\,\,r=e^{v},\,\,r_{0}=e^{w}\,\,{\rm and}\,\,r,r_{0}\in(0,\infty),

(1.22)

B(v):=b(r),\qquad D(v):=d(r),\qquad{\rm where}\,\,r=e^{v},\,\,r\in(0,\infty).

(1.23)

We also define

f_{0}^{k}(v):=\omega_{0}^{k}(r),\quad\phi_{k}(t,v):=\psi_{k}(t,r),\qquad{\rm where}\,\,r=e^{v},\,\,r\in(0,\infty).

(1.24)

It follows from (
1.19
)-(
1.24
) that for
\iota\in\{+,-\},v,w\in\mathbb{R}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
,
\epsilon\in[-1/4,1/4]
,

k^{2}\Pi^{\iota}_{k,\epsilon}(v,w)-\partial_{v}^{2}\Pi^{\iota}_{k,\epsilon}(v,w)+e^{2v}D(v)\frac{\Pi^{\iota}_{k,\epsilon}(v,w)}{B(v)-B(w)+i\iota\epsilon}=e^{2v}\frac{f_{0}^{k}(v)}{B(v)-B(w)+i\iota\epsilon},

(1.25)

and the normalized stream function has the representation formula

\phi_{k}(t,v)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ikB(w)t}\partial_{w}B(w)\left[\Pi_{k,\epsilon}^{-}(v,w)-\Pi_{k,\epsilon}^{+}(v,w)\right]dw.

(1.26)

The key is to study the regularity properties of the associated spectral density functions
\Pi_{k,\epsilon}^{-}(v,w)
and
\Pi_{k,\epsilon}^{+}(v,w)
for
v,w\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\epsilon\in[-1/4,1/4]\backslash\{0\}
small.

We summarize our calculations in the following proposition.

Proposition 1.2
.

Suppose
\Omega(r),r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, is a radial function satisfying the assumption (
1.10
). Let
U(r),r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, be given through

\partial_{r}U(r)+U(r)/r=\Omega(r),\,\,r\in(0,\infty),\quad{\rm with}\,\,\lim_{r\to 0}U(r)=\lim_{r\to\infty}U(r)=0.

(1.27)

Consider
\Omega(r)
as the steady vorticity profile for the two dimensional incompressible Euler equation with the associated velocity field
U(r)e_{\theta}
. The linearized equations around
\Omega
for the perturbation vorticity
\omega(t,\theta,r)\in C^{1}([0,\infty),H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+},r\langle r\rangle^{8}d\theta dr))
and the associated stream function
\psi(t,\theta,r)
,
t\geq 0,\theta\in\mathbb{T},r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
are given by

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\omega+\frac{U(r)}{r}\partial_{\theta}\omega-\frac{\Omega^{\prime}(r)}{r}\partial_{\theta}\psi=0,\quad\left(\partial_{rr}+r^{-1}\partial_{r}+r^{-2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}\right)\psi=\omega.\end{split}

(1.28)

We assume that the initial vorticity deviation
\omega_{0}(\theta,r)
satisfies the orthogonality conditions

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{0}(\theta,r)(\cos{\theta},\sin{\theta})\,r^{2}drd\theta=0,\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\int_{\mathbb{T}}\omega_{0}(\theta,r)\,d\theta=0.

(1.29)

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
and
t\geq 0,r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, letting

\omega_{k}(t,r):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\omega(t,\theta,r)e^{-ik\theta}\,d\theta,\quad\psi_{k}(t,r):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\psi(t,\theta,r)e^{-ik\theta}\,d\theta,

(1.30)

then
\omega_{k}(t,r),\psi_{k}(t,r)
satisfy for
t\geq 0,r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\omega_{k}+ik\frac{U(r)}{r}\omega_{k}-ik\frac{\Omega^{\prime}(r)}{r}\psi_{k}=0,\quad\left(\partial_{rr}+r^{-1}\partial_{r}-k^{2}r^{-2}\right)\psi_{k}=\omega_{k}.\end{split}

(1.31)

Define for
t\geq 0,r\in\mathbb{R}^{+},v\in\mathbb{R}
with
r=e^{v}
, the functions

\begin{split}&b(r):=U(r)/r,\quad d(r):=\Omega^{\prime}(r)/r,\quad B(v):=b(r),\quad D(v):=d(r),\\
&f_{k}(t,v):=\omega_{k}(t,r),\quad f_{0}^{k}(v):=\omega_{0}^{k}(r),\quad\phi_{k}(t,v):=\psi_{k}(t,r).\end{split}

(1.32)

We have the representation formula for
t\geq 0,v\in\mathbb{R}
,

\phi_{k}(t,v)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ikB(w)t}\partial_{w}B(w)\left[\Pi_{k,\epsilon}^{-}(v,w)-\Pi_{k,\epsilon}^{+}(v,w)\right]dw,

(1.33)

where the spectral density functions
\Pi_{k,\epsilon}^{\iota}(v,w)
satisfy for
\iota\in\{+,-\},v,w\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\epsilon\in[-1/8,1/8]\backslash\{0\}

k^{2}\Pi^{\iota}_{k,\epsilon}(v,w)-\partial_{v}^{2}\Pi^{\iota}_{k,\epsilon}(v,w)+e^{2v}D(v)\frac{\Pi^{\iota}_{k,\epsilon}(v,w)}{B(v)-B(w)+i\iota\epsilon}=\frac{e^{2v}f_{0}^{k}(v)}{B(v)-B(w)+i\iota\epsilon}.

(1.34)

We also record the following bounds for later applications. There exists
C^{\ast}\in(0,\infty)
, depending on
C_{\ast},c_{\ast}
in (
1.10
) such that for
v\in\mathbb{R}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[1,\infty)
,

\begin{split}&\Big|D(v)-\frac{c_{\ast}}{(1+e^{2v})^{4}}\Big|\leq C^{\ast}\frac{e^{2v}}{(1+e^{2v})^{5}},\quad\big|\partial^{j}_{v}D(v)\big|\leq(C^{\ast})^{j}(j!)^{2}\frac{e^{2v}}{(1+e^{2v})^{5}},\\
&B(v)\approx\frac{1}{1+e^{2v}},\quad\partial_{v}B(v)\approx-\frac{e^{2v}}{(1+e^{2v})^{2}},\quad\big|\partial^{j}_{v}B(v)\big|\lesssim(C^{\ast})^{j}(j!)^{2}\frac{e^{2v}}{(1+e^{2v})^{2}},\end{split}

(1.35)

\partial_{v}B(v)=(c_{\ast}/4)e^{2v}+O\big(e^{4v}\big)\qquad{\rm for}\,\,v<0.

(1.36)

1.3.
Main results

Fix
k^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[5,\infty)
. Denote
\alpha\wedge\beta:=\min\{\alpha,\beta\}
for
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Define for
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
, the numbers
\varkappa_{k}:=|k|\wedge k^{\dagger}
,
\mu_{k}:=\sqrt{k^{2}+8}
,
\mu_{k}^{\ast}:=\frac{9\mu_{k}+|k|+2}{10}
, and for
v,\rho,w_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
the function
d_{w_{\ast}}(v,\rho)
as

d_{w_{\ast}}(v,\rho):=\big|\big[\min\{v,\rho\},\max\{v,\rho\}\big]\cap\big[\min\{w_{\ast},0\},0\big]\big|.

(1.37)

Define also the main weight functions
\varpi_{k,w_{\ast}}(v,\rho)
and
\zeta_{k,w_{\ast}}(v)
for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
, which are useful in characterizing decay property of the spectral density function and Green’s functions below, as the following: for
v,\rho,w_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\varpi_{k,w_{\ast}}(v,\rho):=e^{-|k||v-\rho|-(\mu_{k}-|k|)d_{w_{\ast}}(v,\rho)},\quad\zeta_{k,w_{\ast}}(v,\rho):=\frac{1}{\varpi_{k,w_{\ast}}(v,\rho)}.

(1.38)

We take two small constants
0<\delta_{1}\ll\delta_{0}
which will be used to quantify the Gevrey regularity below.
\delta_{0}
is determined by the regularity of the background flow
B(v)
, and
\delta_{1}
is related to the regularity of the initial data. For our purposes, we assume that the background flow is much smoother than the solution to the linearized equation we consider. (Hence the condition that
\delta_{0}\gg\delta_{1}>0
.)

Fix
\Phi_{0}(v)\in C^{\infty}(-\infty,-1)
such that
\Phi_{0}\equiv 1
on
(-\infty,-2]
and

\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\big|e^{\langle\xi\rangle^{8/9}}\widehat{\partial_{v}\Phi_{0}}(\xi)\big|\lesssim 1.

We choose also smooth cutoff functions
\Phi^{\ast},\Phi^{\ast\ast}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
satisfying
\Phi^{\ast}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(-4,4)
and
\Phi^{\ast}\equiv 1
on
[-2,2]
,
\Phi^{\ast\ast}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(-5,5)
and
\Phi^{\ast\ast}\equiv 1
on
[-4,4]
, and

\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}e^{\langle\xi\rangle^{8/9}}\Big[\big|\widehat{\,\,\Phi^{\ast}}(\xi)\big|+\big|\widehat{\,\,\,\Phi^{\ast\ast}}(\xi)\big|\Big]\lesssim 1.

We make the following assumptions on the initial data.

Assumption 1.3
.

There exist coefficients
\sigma_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
with
\sigma_{k}=0
for
|k|\geq k^{\dagger}+1
, and constants
M^{\dagger}_{k}\in(0,\infty)
, such that the following statement holds. Define for
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}
,

F_{0k}(v):=f_{0}^{k}(v)-(\sigma_{k}/c_{\ast})D(v)e^{|k|v}\Phi_{0}(v),\qquad{\rm and}\qquad F^{j}_{0k}(v):=F_{0k}(v)\Phi^{\ast}(v-j),

(1.39)

then
F_{0k}^{j}
satisfies the bounds for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\},j\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\left\|e^{\delta_{1}\langle k,\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\widehat{\,F^{j}_{0k}}(\xi)\right\|_{L^{2}(\xi\in\mathbb{R})}\leq M^{\dagger}_{k}\frac{e^{\mu^{\ast}_{\varkappa_{k}}j}}{1+e^{(\mu^{\ast}_{\varkappa_{k}}+\varkappa_{k}+8)j}}.\\

(1.40)

We briefly comment on the assumption (
1.40
). Notice that (
1.40
) contains conditions both on the regularity and decay of
F_{0k}(v)
. The decay of
F_{0k}(v)
is assumed to be of the order
e^{-(\varkappa_{k}+8)v}
as
v\to+\infty
, and
e^{\mu^{\ast}_{\varkappa_{k}}v}
as
v\to-\infty
. These decay conditions are compatible with the expected decay of smooth, fast decaying initial data
\omega_{0}(x,y)
.
\mu_{\varkappa_{k}}^{\ast}
needs to be slightly bigger than
\mu_{\varkappa_{k}}
(when
|k|\geq 2
) which is the index of decay for the solution, see (
1.57
). This does not seem to be a problem since the nonlinear interactions will only produce terms of faster decay. On the other hand, the decay as
v\to+\infty
is not a concern for us thanks to the fast decay of
D(v)
as
v\to+\infty
.

Decompose for
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
,

\Pi_{k,\epsilon}^{\iota}(v,w):=(\sigma_{k}/c_{\ast})e^{|k|v}\Phi_{0}(v)+\Gamma_{k,\epsilon}^{\iota}(v,w).

(1.41)

It follows from (
1.34
) that
\Gamma_{k,\epsilon}^{\iota}(v,w)
satisfies the equation for
v,w\in\mathbb{R},\epsilon\in[-1/8,1/8]\backslash\{0\},

\iota\in\{+,-\}
,

\begin{split}&(k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})\Gamma_{k,\epsilon}^{\iota}(v,w)+\frac{e^{2v}D(v)}{B(v)-B(w)+i\iota\epsilon}\Gamma_{k,\epsilon}^{\iota}(v,w)\\
&=\frac{e^{2v}F_{0k}(v)}{B(v)-B(w)+i\iota\epsilon}+(\sigma_{k}/c_{\ast})\big(2|k|e^{|k|v}\partial_{v}\Phi_{0k}(v)+e^{kv}\partial_{v}^{2}\Phi_{0k}(v)\big).\end{split}

(1.42)

Our main result is the bounds on the profile of the spectral density function. We allow the implied constants to depend on
k^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[5,\infty)
and the background flow (more precisely the constants
c_{\ast},C_{\ast}\in(0,\infty)
appearing in (
1.10
) and the structural constant
\kappa\in(0,1)
coming from the limiting absorption principle, see section
5
).

Theorem 1.4
.

Assume that
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
,
f_{k}(t,v),\phi_{k}(t,v)
for
t\geq 0,v\in\mathbb{R}
are as in proposition
1.2
, and that the assumption
1.3
holds. Then we have the following conclusions. For some sufficiently small
\epsilon_{\ast}\in(0,1)
,

\iota\in\{+,-\}
, and for all
w\in\mathbb{R}
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon_{\ast}e^{-2|w|}
, we have

\big\|\Gamma_{k,\epsilon}^{\iota}(v,w)\big\|_{L^{2}(v\in\mathbb{R})}\lesssim(M_{k}^{\dagger}+|\sigma_{k}|)/|k|.

(1.43)

The limiting spectral density function

\Gamma_{k}(v,w):=(-i)\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}\big[\Gamma_{k,\epsilon}^{+}(v,w)-\Gamma_{k,\epsilon}^{-}(v,w)\big]=2\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}\Im\,\Gamma_{k,\epsilon}^{+}(v,w)

(1.44)

exists, as limit of functions in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Define for
v,w\in\mathbb{R}
, the “profile”
\Theta_{k}(v,w)
for
\Gamma_{k}(v,w)
,

\Theta_{k}(v,w):=\Gamma_{k}(v+w,w).

(1.45)

Then
\Theta_{k}(v,w)
satisfies the following properties.

(i) Bounds when
v
is away from
0
. For
w_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R},v_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
, define for
v,w\in\mathbb{R}

\Theta_{k,v_{\ast}}^{w_{\ast}}(v,w):=\Theta_{k}(v,w)\Phi^{\ast}(v-v_{\ast})\Phi^{\ast}(w-w_{\ast})(1-\Phi^{\ast}(v)),

(1.46)

then we have

\begin{split}&\Big\|(|k|+|\xi|)\Big[e^{\delta_{0}\langle k,\xi\rangle^{1/2}}+e^{\delta_{1}\langle k,\eta\rangle^{1/2}}\Big]\widehat{\,\,\Theta_{k,v_{\ast}}^{w_{\ast}}}(\xi,\eta)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R})}\\
&\lesssim\big(M_{k}^{\dagger}+|\sigma_{k}|\big)\varpi_{\varkappa_{k},w_{\ast}}(w_{\ast},0)\varpi_{\varkappa_{k},w_{\ast}}(v_{\ast}+w_{\ast},w_{\ast}).\end{split}

(1.47)

(ii) Bounds when
v
is close to
0
. For
w_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
, define for
v,w\in\mathbb{R}
,

\Theta_{k}^{w_{\ast}}(v,w):=\Theta_{k}(v,w)\Phi^{\ast}(w-w_{\ast})\Phi^{\ast}(v),

(1.48)

we have

\begin{split}&\Big\|\big(|k|+|\xi|\big)e^{\delta_{1}\langle k,\eta\rangle^{1/2}}\widehat{\,\,\,\Theta_{k}^{w_{\ast}}}(\xi,\eta)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R})}\lesssim(M_{k}^{\dagger}+|\sigma_{k}|)\varpi_{\varkappa_{k},w_{\ast}}(w_{\ast},0).\end{split}

(1.49)

(iii) Equation for
\Theta_{k}
. In addition,
\Theta_{k}(v,w)
satisfies for
v,w\in\mathbb{R}
the equation

(k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})\Theta_{k}(v,w)+{\rm P.V.}\frac{e^{2v+2w}D(v+w)\Theta_{k}(v,w)}{B(v+w)-B(w)}=-2\pi\frac{e^{2w}\big(D(w)\digamma_{k}(w)-F_{0k}(w)\big)}{B^{\prime}(w)}\delta(v),

(1.50)

where
{\rm P.V.}
represents principal value and
\digamma_{k}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies the bound, for
w_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\digamma_{k}^{w_{\ast}}(w):=\digamma_{k}(w)\Phi^{\ast}(w-w_{\ast})
,

\Big\|e^{\delta_{1}\langle k,\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\widehat{\,\,\digamma_{k}^{w_{\ast}}}(\xi)\Big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim(M_{k}^{\dagger}+|\sigma_{k}|)\varpi_{\varkappa_{k},w_{\ast}}(w_{\ast},0).

(1.51)

(iv) Refined regularity property of
\Theta_{k}(v,w)
in
v
. Moreover, for any
\zeta:[-10,10]\to\mathbb{R}
with Gevrey-2 regularity and
|\zeta^{\prime}|\gtrsim 1
on
[-10,10]
, more precisely
\zeta\in\widetilde{G}_{M}^{1/2}(-10,10)
for some
M\in(0,\infty)
(see (
2.6
) for the precise definition of the Gevrey space
\widetilde{G}_{M}^{1/2}
) then for
v,\rho\in[-4,4]
, the function
\Theta(\zeta(v+\rho)-\zeta(\rho),w)
is Gevrey regular in
\rho,w
. More precisely, for any
w_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
, define

\mathcal{H}_{w_{\ast}}(v,\rho,w):=\Theta(\zeta(v+\rho)-\zeta(\rho),w)\Phi^{\ast}(w-w_{\ast})\Phi_{k}^{\ast}(v)\Phi^{\ast}(\rho),

(1.52)

then for some
\delta_{1}^{\prime}\in(0,1)
depending on
M
and
\delta_{1}
we have

\begin{split}&\Big\|\big(|k|+|\alpha|\big)\Big[e^{\delta_{1}^{\prime}\langle k,\xi\rangle^{1/2}}+e^{\delta_{1}\langle k,\eta\rangle^{1/2}}\Big]\widehat{\,\,\mathcal{H}_{w_{\ast}}}(\alpha,\xi,\eta)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\lesssim(M_{k}^{\dagger}+|\sigma_{k}|)\varpi_{\varkappa_{k},w_{\ast}}(w_{\ast},0).\end{split}

(1.53)

(v) Representation formula for the stream function and vorticity function. Finally, we have the representation formula

\begin{split}&\phi_{k}(t,v)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ikB(w)t}\Theta_{k}(v-w,w)B^{\prime}(w)\,dw,\qquad f_{k}(t,v)=-e^{-2v}(k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})\phi_{k}(t,v).\\
\end{split}

(1.54)

Using the bounds on the spectral density function, we can prove the following result on the evolution of
f_{k}(t,v)
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Decompose

\begin{split}f_{k}(t,v)&=f_{k}^{1}(t,v)+f_{k}^{2}(t,v)\\
&:=\frac{e^{-2v}}{2\pi}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-iktB(w)}(k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})\Theta_{k}(v-w,w)\Phi^{\ast}(v-w)B^{\prime}(w)\,dw\\
&+\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-iktB(w)}(k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})\Theta_{k}(v-w,w)(1-\Phi^{\ast}(v-w))B^{\prime}(w)\,dw\bigg],\end{split}

(1.55)

then
f_{k}^{1}(t,v)e^{ikB(v)t}
is Gevrey smooth in
v
uniformly over
t\in[0,\infty)
, and
f_{k}^{2}(t,v)
is Gevrey smooth in
v
and decays in
t
.

More precisely, there exist
\delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}\in(0,\infty)
depending on
\delta_{0}
, and
\delta_{1}^{\prime\prime}\in(0,1)
depending on
\delta_{1}
, such that for any
v_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
, defining

F_{k,v_{\ast}}^{1}(t,v):=f_{k}^{1}(t,v)e^{ikB(v)t}\Phi^{\ast}(v-v_{\ast}),\qquad F_{k,v_{\ast}}^{2}(t,v):=f_{k}^{2}(t,v)\Phi^{\ast}(v-v_{\ast}),

(1.56)

we have the bounds for all
t\geq 0
,

\begin{split}&\Big\|e^{\delta_{1}^{\prime\prime}\langle k,\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\widehat{\,\,F_{k,v_{\ast}}^{1}}(t,\xi)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim(M_{k}^{\dagger}+|\sigma_{k}|)\Big[e^{-\mu_{\varkappa_{k}}|v_{\ast}|}{\bf 1}_{v_{\ast}<0}+e^{-(\varkappa_{k}+8)|v_{\ast}|}{\bf 1}_{v_{\ast}>0}\Big],\\
&\big|B^{\prime}(v_{\ast})\big|\Big\|e^{\delta_{0}^{\prime\prime}\langle k,\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\widehat{\,\,F_{k,v_{\ast}}^{2}}(t,\xi)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\frac{M_{k}^{\dagger}+|\sigma_{k}|}{\langle t\rangle}\Big[e^{-\mu_{\varkappa_{k}}|v_{\ast}|}{\bf 1}_{v_{\ast}<0}+e^{-(\varkappa_{k}+8)|v_{\ast}|}{\bf 1}_{v_{\ast}>0}\Big].\end{split}

(1.57)

1.4.
Remarks on the main theorems

We briefly discuss some of the conclusions in our main theorems
1.4
-
1.5
.

(i) As discussed earlier, our main objective is to study the spectral density function
\Gamma_{k}(v,w)
and more precisely its “profile”
\Theta_{k}(v,w)
which is smooth in
w\in\mathbb{R}
(but not in
v\in\mathbb{R}
). The crucial points in the bounds (
1.47
) and (
1.49
) are that
\Theta_{k}(v,w)
is as smooth in
w
as the initial data
F_{0k}
, in Gevrey spaces. The exact weight
e^{\delta_{1}\langle k,\xi\rangle^{1/2}}
is at the right regularity required for nonlinear inviscid damping, see
[
4
,
14
,
21
,
36
]
. The bounds (
1.53
) provide more refined information on the regularity of
\Theta_{k}(v,w)
in
v
. Notice that
\Theta_{k}(v,w)
is not smooth in
v
. (
1.53
) shows that, however,
\Theta(\zeta(v+\rho)-\zeta(\rho),w)
is smooth in
\rho
, if
\zeta
satisfies the natural assumptions in (iv) of Theorem
1.4
.

(ii) The decomposition (
1.55
)-(
1.56
) shows that in general we do not have a uniform profile that we can control over all times. However, as it can be seen from the bounds (
1.57
) the “nonlocal profile”
F_{k,v_{\ast}}^{2}(t,v)
is much smoother than the initial data and its regularity depends only on the smoothness of the background flow. In addition, the nonlocal profile
F_{k,v_{\ast}}^{2}(t,v)
decays over time. This remarkable property may be important for the nonlinear analysis.

(iii) In the formulation of the bounds (
1.47
) and (
1.49
), we included suitable decay in space. Such physical space decay can be improved at the expense of sacrificing the amount of regularity we can prove. For instance we can capture the decay
e^{-|k||v|}
precisely if we work with the weight
e^{C\langle\xi/k\rangle^{1/2}}
. However, our belief is that, for the nonlinear analysis, it is more important to work with the right regularity space characterized by the weight
e^{\delta_{1}\langle k,\xi\rangle^{1/2}}
, than with the best physical space decay, especially for large
k
.

(iv) It follows from (
1.55
)-(
1.57
) that
f_{k}(t,v)\rightharpoonup 0
as
t\to\infty
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
, which is at the heart of the inviscid damping phenomenon.

(v) The order of decay
e^{-\mu_{\varkappa_{k}}|v_{\ast}|}=e^{-\sqrt{k^{2}+8}\,|v_{\ast}|}
for
|k|\leq k^{\dagger}
as
v_{\ast}\to-\infty
, in (
1.57
) for
F_{k,v_{\ast}}^{1}
, is faster than the expected rate
e^{-|k||v_{\ast}|}
, and is a manifestation of the vorticity depletion phenomenon. We note that the index for decay
\sqrt{k^{2}+8}
we obtained is slightly smaller than
|k|+2
obtained in
[
5
]
. Incidentally in the explicitly solvable case
|k|=1
, we have
\sqrt{k^{2}+8}=|k|+2
. It is not clear to us if the optimal rate of enhanced decay for
F_{k,v_{\ast}}^{1}
is important for the nonlinear analysis at this time.

1.5.
Main ideas of proof for Theorem
1.4

The basic idea is to study the equation (
1.42
) and obtain coercive bounds using the the limiting absorption principle. This has been done in many works by now, see e.g.
[
24
,
51
]
. A new feature in the case of vortices is that for
w\ll-1
, the potential
\frac{e^{2v}D(v)}{B(v)-B(w)+i\iota\epsilon}\approx 8
for
v\in[w,0]
which is a long interval, as can be seen from simple calculations using (
1.35
)-(
1.36
). Therefore we need to absorb part of the potential into the main term when solving (
1.42
). As a result, we have to study a new Green’s function adapted to the nonlocal potential, see section
3
.

Once we obtained the necessary bounds in weighted Sobolev spaces, we can use the commutator argument to obtain the desired Gevrey regularity, as in
[
25
]
. The implementation here is, however, much more complicated since we need to work with weighted space and the Green’s function is not explicitly given. The key is to obtain refined bounds in Gevrey space for the Green’s function associated with the long range potential for
w\leq-10
, see proposition
3.4
, which captures both the crucial Gevrey regularity property and optimal physical space decay of the Green’s function simultaneously.

Another interesting feature is that the limit density function
\Gamma_{k}(v,w)
enjoys better decay property than
\Gamma^{\iota}_{k,\epsilon}(v,w)
. The main reason is that in equation (
1.42
) the right hand side is mostly “real valued” and that implies
\Gamma^{\iota}_{k,\epsilon}(v,w)
has “small” imaginary part, which can also be seen from equation (
1.50
).

1.6.
Organization of the paper

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In section
2
we review some technical results on Gevrey spaces and elliptic regularity theory in Gevrey spaces. In section
3
we study the Green’s function associated with a long range potential that plays an important role in the analysis of the spectral density function
\Gamma_{k}(v,w)
for
w\leq 0
. In section
4
, we study the spectrum of the linearized operator which is essential for proving the limiting absorption principle. In section
5
, we prove the limiting absorption principle that will be used to prove bounds on
\Gamma_{k,\epsilon}^{\iota}(v,w)
in weighted Sobolev spaces. In section
6
we consider the special case when
|k|=1
, which remarkably is explicitly solvable. In section
7
and section
8
, we apply the limiting absorption and use the commutator argument to bound
\Gamma_{k,\epsilon}^{\iota}(v,w)
and
\Gamma_{k}(v,w)
. In section
9
we assemble all the bounds and prove theorem
1.4
and theorem
1.5
.

2.
Preliminaries on Gevrey spaces and elliptic Gevrey regularity theory

2.1.
Notations and conventions

We summarize here some of our main notation. We use the weight
e^{C\delta_{0}\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2}}
to characterize the regularity of functions that depend only on the background flow and smoother Gevrey cutoff functions, and
\delta_{0}
is chosen sufficiently small depending on the regularity of the background flow. We also allow the implied constants to depend on the background flow which is fixed. We fix
k^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[5,\infty)
throughout the paper, and set for
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
,
\varkappa_{k}:=\min\{|k|,k^{\dagger}\}
. We also set
\mu_{k}:=\sqrt{k^{2}+8}
and
\mu_{k}^{\ast}:=\frac{9\mu_{k}+|k|+2}{10}
. Notice that
\mu_{k}<\mu_{k}^{\ast}\leq|k|+2
for
|k|\geq 2
, and
\mu_{k}=\mu_{1}^{\ast}=3
.

2.1.1.
Fourier transforms

In this paper, we use
\widehat{h}
to denote the Fourier transform of
h
. If
h
is a function of many variables and when we need to take Fourier transform in some but not all of the variables, we shall use
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\xi,\eta
to indicate the variable after Fourier transform, and
\rho,v,w
to indicate that we do not take Fourier transform in these variables. For example, suppose
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, then

\widehat{h}(\rho,\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(\rho,w)e^{-iw\xi}\,dw.

(2.1)

2.1.2.
Commutator arguments

In this paper we use commutator arguments extensively to obtain higher regularity (mostly Gevrey regularity) estimates, starting from a low regularity (mostly Sobolev regularity) estimate. Suppose the Fourier multiplier we use to characterize the high regularity is
A(\xi):=e^{C\langle m,\xi\rangle^{1/2}}
,
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
for some
C\in(0,\infty),m\in\mathbb{R}
, we often need to assume qualitatively

\big\|A(\xi)\widehat{h}(\xi)\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}<\infty,

(2.2)

to obtain quantitatively

\big\|A(\xi)\widehat{h}(\xi)\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim 1.

(2.3)

To remove the qualitative assumption (
2.2
), we can for example follow the technique in the appendix of
[
22
]
and introduce for
\rho\gg 1
,

\sigma_{\rho}(r):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{2}r^{-1/2}-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{-1/2}&{\rm if}\,\,r\in(0,\rho]\\
0&{\rm if}\,\,r\geq\rho,\end{array}\right.,\qquad\Pi_{\rho}(r):=\int_{0}^{r}\sigma_{\rho}(x)\,dx,

(2.4)

and define for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,

A_{\rho}(\xi):=e^{C\Pi_{\rho}(\langle m,\xi\rangle))}.

(2.5)

Clearly
A_{\rho}(\xi)
is a bounded function (with a bound that depends on
\rho>1
),
A_{\rho}(\xi)\leq A(\xi)
and
A_{\rho}(\xi)\to A(\xi)
as
\rho\to\infty
for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
. The idea is to use
A_{\rho}(\eta)
in the proof of (
2.3
) and then send
\rho\to\infty
. We shall use this convention many times in our proof without going through it every time.

2.2.
Gevrey spaces

We summarize here some general properties of the Gevrey spaces of functions. See
[
44
,
48
]
for more discussion and further references on Gevrey spaces.
To perform certain algebraic operations, it is very useful to have a related definition in the physical space. For any domain
D\subseteq\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
(or
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}
) and parameters
s\in(0,1)
and
M\geq 1
we define the spaces

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{s}_{M}(D):=\big\{f:D\to\mathbb{C}:\,\|f\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{s}_{M}(D)}:=\sup_{x\in D,\,m\geq 0,\,|\alpha|\leq m}|D^{\alpha}f(x)|M^{-m}(m+1)^{-m/s}<\infty\big\}.

(2.6)

We start with a lemma connecting the space
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{s}_{M}
with the characterization of Gevrey spaces using Fourier transforms.

Lemma 2.1
.

[
22
, Subsection A.1]

(i) Suppose that
s\in(0,1)
,
K>1
, and
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
with
{\rm supp}\,f\subseteq\mathbb{T}\times[-L,L]
satisfies the bounds
\|f\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{s}_{K}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})}\leq 1
.
Then there is
\mu=\mu(K,s)>0
such that

\big|\widehat{f}(k,\xi)\big|\lesssim_{K,s}Le^{-\mu|k,\xi|^{s}}\qquad\text{ for all }k\in\mathbb{Z},\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}.

(2.7)

(ii) Conversely, if
\mu>0
and
s\in(0,1)
, then there is
K=K(s,\mu)>1
such that

\big\|f\big\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{s}_{K}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})}\lesssim_{\mu,s}\big\|e^{\mu\langle k,\xi\rangle^{s}}\widehat{f}(k,\xi)\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R})}.

(2.8)

Using this lemma one can construct cutoff functions in Gevrey spaces: for any points
a^{\prime}<a\leq b<b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and any
s\in(0,1)
there are functions
\Psi
supported in
[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]
, equal to
1
in
[a,b]
, and satisfying
\big|\widetilde{\Psi}(\xi)\big|\lesssim e^{-\langle\xi\rangle^{s}}
for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
. See
[
22
, Subsection A.1]
for an explicit construction of such functions, as well as an elementary proof of Lemma
2.1
. We use several functions of this type in the proof of our main theorem.

The physical space characterization of Gevrey functions is useful when studying compositions and algebraic operations of functions.

Lemma 2.2
.

(i) Assume
s\in(0,1)
,
M\geq 1
, and
f_{1},f_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{s}_{M}(D)
. Then
f_{1}f_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{s}_{M^{\prime}}(D)
and

\|f_{1}f_{2}\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{s}_{M^{\prime}}(D)}\lesssim\|f_{1}\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{s}_{M}(D)}\|f_{2}\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{s}_{M}(D)}

for some
M^{\prime}=M^{\prime}(s,M)\geq M
. Similarly, if
f_{1}\geq 1
in
D
then
\|(1/f_{1})\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{s}_{M^{\prime}}(D)}\lesssim 1
.

(ii) Suppose
s\in(0,1)
,
M\geq 1
,
I_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
is an interval, and
g:\mathbb{T}\times I_{1}\to\mathbb{T}\times I_{2}
satisfies

|D^{\alpha}g(x)|\leq M^{m}(m+1)^{m/s}\qquad\text{ for any }x\in\mathbb{T}\times I_{1},\,m\geq 1,\text{ and }|\alpha|\in[1,m].

(2.9)

If
K\geq 1
and
f\in\widetilde{G}^{s}_{K}(\mathbb{T}\times I_{2})
then
f\circ g\in\widetilde{G}^{s}_{L}(\mathbb{T}\times I_{1})
for some
L=L(s,K,M)\geq 1
and

\left\|f\circ g\right\|_{\widetilde{G}^{s}_{L}(\mathbb{T}\times I_{1})}\lesssim_{s,K,M}\left\|f\right\|_{\widetilde{G}^{s}_{K}(\mathbb{T}\times I_{2})}.

(2.10)

(iii) Assume
s\in(0,1)
,
L\in[1,\infty)
,
I,J\subseteq\mathbb{R}
are open intervals, and
g:I\to J
is a smooth bijective map satisfying, for any
m\geq 1
,

|D^{\alpha}g(x)|\leq L^{m}(m+1)^{m/s}\qquad\text{ for any }x\in I\text{ and }|\alpha|\in[1,m].

(2.11)

If
|g^{\prime}(x)|\geq\rho>0
for any
x\in I
then the inverse function
g^{-1}:J\to I
satisfies the bounds

|D^{\alpha}(g^{-1})(x)|\leq M^{m}(m+1)^{m/s}\qquad\text{ for any }x\in J\text{ and }|\alpha|\in[1,m],

(2.12)

for some constant
M=M(s,L,\rho)\geq L
.

Lemma
2.2
can be proved by elementary means using just the definition (
2.6
). See also
[
48
, Theorems 6.1 and 3.2]
for more general estimates on functions in Gevrey spaces.

For applications below, we prove the following bound on a Fourier transform.

Lemma 2.3
.

Assume that
w_{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
,
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}
with
0<|\epsilon|<e^{-2|w_{\ast}|}
, and
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(-10,10)
with

\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R},\sigma\in\{1,2\}}\big|e^{\langle\xi\rangle^{5/6}}\widehat{\,\Psi_{\sigma}}(\xi)\big|\lesssim 1.

(i) For
\eta,\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, define

h_{1}(\eta,\xi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Psi_{1}(\rho)\frac{1}{B(\rho+w)-B(w)+i\epsilon}\Psi_{2}(w-w_{\ast})e^{-i\rho\eta-iw\xi}\,d\rho dw.

(2.13)

Then we have the bounds

\sup_{\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\big|e^{\delta_{0}\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2}}h(\eta,\xi)\big|\lesssim e^{2|w_{\ast}|}.

(2.14)

(ii) Define for
|\rho|\geq 1/4
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,

h_{2}(\rho,\xi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{B(\rho+w)-B(w)+i\epsilon}\Psi_{1}(w-w_{\ast})e^{-iw\xi}\,dw.

(2.15)

Then we have the bounds

\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\big|e^{\delta_{0}\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2}}h_{2}(\rho,\xi)\big|\lesssim\Big|\frac{1}{B(\rho+w_{\ast})-B(w_{\ast})+i\epsilon}\Big|.

(2.16)

Proof.

Let us assume
\epsilon<0
without loss of generality. Note the identity for
\rho\in\mathbb{R},w\in\mathbb{R}
,

B(\rho+w)-B(w)=\rho\int_{0}^{1}B^{\prime}(w+s\rho)\,ds.

(2.17)

Setting for
|\rho|\leq 20
,
|w-w_{\ast}|\leq 20
,

\delta:=\epsilon/B^{\prime}(w_{\ast})>0,\qquad\psi(\rho,w):=\frac{1}{\int_{0}^{1}\big[B^{\prime}(w+s\rho)/B^{\prime}(w_{\ast})\big]\,ds}\gtrsim 1,

(2.18)

we can write

\begin{split}\frac{B^{\prime}(w_{\ast})}{B(\rho)-B(w)+i\epsilon}=\psi(\rho,w)\frac{1}{\rho+i\delta\psi(\rho,w)}=\frac{1}{i}\psi(\rho,w)\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\delta\psi(\rho,w)\gamma+i\rho\gamma}{\bf 1}_{\gamma>0}\,d\gamma.\end{split}

(2.19)

We note that
\psi(\rho,w)
is Gevrey-2 regular for
|\rho|\leq 20
,
|w-w_{\ast}|\leq 20
with bounds depending only on the background flow
B
.
By Lemma
2.2
, there exist small constants
c,C\in(0,\infty)
depending only on the background flow, such that for
|\rho|\leq 20
,
|w-w_{\ast}|\leq 20
,
\gamma>0
, and all
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,\infty)
,

\big|\partial_{\rho}^{m_{1}}\partial_{w}^{m_{2}}\big[\psi(\rho,w)e^{-\delta\psi(\rho,w)\gamma}\big]\big|\lesssim e^{-c\delta\gamma}C^{m_{1}+m_{2}}((m_{1}+m_{2})!)^{2}.

(2.20)

Denote for
\rho,w\in\mathbb{R}
and
\gamma>0
,

\psi_{\gamma}(\rho,w):=\psi(\rho,w)\Psi_{1}(\rho)\Psi_{2}(w-w_{\ast})e^{-\delta\psi(\rho,w)\gamma}.

(2.21)

In view of (
2.20
), for
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
sufficiently small depending on the background flow
B
, we can bound for
\gamma>0
,

\sup_{\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}}\big|e^{2\delta_{0}\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}\widehat{\,\,\psi_{\gamma}}(\alpha,\beta)\big|\lesssim 1.

(2.22)

Using (
2.19
) and (
2.22
) we can now bound for
\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}

\begin{split}\big|e^{\delta_{0}\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2}}h(\eta,\xi)\big|&\lesssim e^{2|w_{\ast}|}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{\bf 1}_{\gamma>0}\,e^{\delta_{0}\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\psi_{\gamma}(\rho,w)e^{i\rho\gamma-i\rho\eta-iw\xi}d\rho dwd\gamma\Big|\\
&\lesssim e^{2|w_{\ast}|}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\bf 1}_{\gamma>0}\,e^{\delta_{0}\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\widehat{\,\,\psi_{\gamma}}(\eta-\gamma,\xi)d\gamma\Big|\lesssim e^{2|w_{\ast}|}.\end{split}

(2.23)

This completes the proof of (
2.14
). The proof of (
2.16
) is simpler, and follows from the observation that

\frac{B(\rho+w_{\ast})-B(w_{\ast})+i\iota\epsilon}{B(\rho+w)-B(w)+i\iota\epsilon}

(2.24)

is Gevrey-2 for
|\rho|\geq 1/4,|w-w_{\ast}|\leq 20
with bounds depending only on the background flow, has modulus
\approx 1
, and Lemma
2.2
. We omit the standard details. The lemma is then proved.
∎

2.3.
Gevrey regularity for elliptic equations

We now prove the following elliptic regularity estimates in Gevrey spaces.

Proposition 2.4
.

Fix
s\in(0,1),m\in\mathbb{R},v_{0}\in\mathbb{R},L>0
. Assume that
a\in C^{\infty}(v_{0}-2L,v_{0}+2L)
belongs to
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{s}_{M_{0}}
, more precisely for some
C_{0},M_{0}\in(0,\infty)
, and all
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,\infty)
,

\sup_{v\in(v_{0}-2L,v_{0}+2L)}\Big|(L\partial_{v})^{\alpha}a(v)\Big|\leq C_{0}L^{-2}M_{0}^{\alpha}(\alpha!)^{1/s}.

(2.25)

Suppose that the function
h\in L^{2}(v_{0}-2L,v_{0}+2L)
satisfies

(m^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})h(v)+a(v)h(v)=0,\quad{\rm for}\,\,v\in(v_{0}-2L,v_{0}+2L).

(2.26)

Then
v\in C^{\infty}(v_{0}-2L,v_{0}+2L)
. Moreover, for suitable
M_{1}\in(0,\infty)
(depending on
C_{0},M_{0},s
),
h
satisfies the bounds for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,\infty)
,

\Big\|(L\partial_{v})^{\alpha}h(v)\Big\|_{L^{2}(v_{0}-L/2,v_{0}+L/2)}\leq M_{1}^{\alpha}\big(\alpha!\big)^{1/s}\|h\|_{L^{2}(v_{0}-2L,v_{0}+2L)}e^{-|m|L/20}.

(2.27)

Proof.

By translation invariance and a rescaling, we can assume
v_{0}=0
and
L=1
. Standard elliptic regularity theory shows that
h\in C^{\infty}(-2,2)
, so we only have to prove the quantitative bounds (
2.27
) with
L=1
. Assume that
\|h\|_{L^{2}(-2,2)}=\sigma
. We first prove the easier bounds

\|h(v)\|_{L^{2}(-1,1)}\leq C_{0}^{\prime}M_{0}^{\prime}\,\sigma\,e^{-|m|/20},

(2.28)

for suitable
C_{0}^{\prime},M_{0}^{\prime}\in(0,\infty)
depending on
C_{0},M_{0}
. The bounds (
2.28
) follow from a simple comparison argument. Indeed, we can find
c_{1}\in(-2,-31/16),c_{2}\in(31/16,2)
such that
|h(c_{1})|\leq 5\sigma,|h(c_{2})|\leq 5\sigma
. Since (
2.28
) is nontrivial only for large
|m|
, can we assume that
m^{2}-|a(v)|\geq m^{2}/2
for
v\in[-2,2]
. The desired bounds (
2.28
) then follow from comparing
h
with the solution
h^{\ast}
to

(m^{2}/2-\partial_{v}^{2})h^{\ast}(v)=0,\quad{\rm for}\,\,v\in[c_{1},c_{2}],\quad{\rm with}\,\,h^{\ast}(c_{1})=5\sigma,\,\,h^{\ast}(c_{2})=5\sigma.

It then suffices to prove for suitable
M_{1}\in(0,\infty)
(depending on
C_{0},M_{0},s
),
h
satisfies the bounds for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,\infty)
,

\Big\|\partial_{v}^{j}h(v)\Big\|_{L^{2}(-1/2,1/2)}\leq M_{1}^{j}\big(j!\big)^{1/s}\|h\|_{L^{2}(-1,1)}.

(2.29)

We assume
\|h\|_{L^{2}(-1,1)}=1
and use an induction argument. Fix
\delta\in(0,(1/s)-1)
. Set for
j\geq 0
,

r_{j}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2(9+j)^{\delta}}.

(2.30)

Choose smooth cutoff function
\varphi_{j}(v)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(-r_{j},r_{j})
,
j\geq 0
such that
\varphi_{j}\equiv 1
on
[-r_{j+1},r_{j+1}]
,
|\nabla\varphi_{j}|\leq 4\delta^{-1}(10+j)^{1+\delta}
. We shall prove by induction that for suitable
M_{1}\in(1,\infty)
depending on
C_{0},M_{0},s
, it holds that for all
j\geq 0
,

\left\|\partial_{v}^{j}h\right\|_{L^{2}(-r_{j},r_{j})}\leq M_{1}^{j}(j!)^{1/s}.

(2.31)

It is clear that (
2.31
) holds for
j=0
. Suppose that (
2.31
) holds for
j\leq j_{0}
, for some
j_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,\infty)
, we need to prove that (
2.31
) holds also for
j=j_{0}+1
. Taking
\partial_{v}^{j_{0}}
to (
2.26
) we obtain that for
v\in(-1,1)
,

(m^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})\partial_{v}^{j_{0}}h+\partial_{v}^{j_{0}}\big(a(v)h\big)=0.

(2.32)

Therefore by integrating against
\partial_{v}^{j_{0}}h\,\varphi_{j_{0}}^{2}
and using induction assumption, we obtain that

\begin{split}&\int_{-1}^{1}m^{2}\big|\partial_{v}^{j_{0}}h\big|^{2}\varphi_{j_{0}}^{2}+\big|\partial_{v}^{j_{0}+1}h\big|^{2}\varphi_{j_{0}}^{2}\,dv\\
&\leq\left|2\int_{-1}^{1}\partial_{v}^{j_{0}+1}h\,\partial_{v}\varphi_{j_{0}}\varphi_{j_{0}}\partial_{v}^{j_{0}}h\,dv\right|+\sum_{j=0}^{j_{0}}\frac{j_{0}!}{j!(j_{0}-j)!}\int_{-1}^{1}\left|\partial_{v}^{j}a(v)\partial_{v}^{j_{0}-j}h\,\partial_{v}^{j_{0}}h\right|\varphi_{j_{0}}^{2}\,dv\\
&\leq\frac{1}{4}\int_{-1}^{1}\left|\partial_{v}^{j_{0}+1}h\right|^{2}\varphi_{j_{0}}^{2}\,dv+64\delta^{-2}(10+j_{0})^{2+2\delta}M_{1}^{2j_{0}}(j_{0}!)^{2/s}\\
&\quad+\sum_{j=0}^{j_{0}}\frac{j_{0}!}{j!(j_{0}-j)!}C_{0}M_{0}^{j}(j!)^{1/s}M_{1}^{j_{0}-j}\big((j_{0}-j)!\big)^{1/s}M_{1}^{j_{0}}(j_{0}!)^{1/s}.\end{split}

(2.33)

Observe that if we choose
M_{1}>M_{0}
, then for
j_{0}\geq 0,j\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,j_{0}]
,

\frac{j_{0}!}{j!(j_{0}-j)!}M_{0}^{j}(j!)^{1/s}M_{1}^{j_{0}-j}\big((j_{0}-j)!\big)^{1/s}\leq M_{1}^{j_{0}}(j_{0}!)^{1/s}.

(2.34)

It follows from (
2.33
)-(
2.34
) that

\int_{-1}^{1}\left|\partial_{v}^{j_{0}+1}h\right|^{2}\varphi_{j_{0}}^{2}\,dv\leq 100\delta^{-2}(10+j_{0})^{2+2\delta}M_{1}^{2j_{0}}(j_{0}!)^{2/s}+2C_{0}M_{1}^{2j_{0}}(j_{0}!)^{2/s}(j_{0}+1).

(2.35)

To conclude the proof, recalling that
\delta\in(0,1/s-1)
, we only need to choose
M_{1}>M_{0}
sufficiently large such that for all
j_{0}\geq 0

2C_{0}(j_{0}+1)+100\delta^{-2}(10+j_{0})^{2+2\delta}\leq M_{1}^{2}(j_{0}+1)^{2/s}.

(2.36)

The proof is then complete.
∎

We shall also need the following lemma on elliptic estimates for later applications.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\alpha\in[1/100,\infty)
. Suppose that
L>0
and the potential
a(v)
satisfies for some
C_{2},M_{2}\in(0,\infty)
,

\sup_{v\in[-2L,2L]}\big|(L\partial_{v})^{j}a(v)\big|\leq C_{2}L^{-2}M_{2}^{j}(j!)^{2}.

(2.37)

Then for sufficiently small
\delta\in(0,\infty)
depending on
C_{2},M_{2}
the following statement holds.
Fix smooth cutoff functions
\Psi,\Psi^{\ast}
with

\begin{split}&{\rm supp}\,\Psi\subset(-3/4,3/4),\quad\Psi\equiv 1\,\,{\rm for}\,\,v\in[-1/2,1/2],\quad\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\big|e^{\langle\xi\rangle^{3/4}}\widehat{\,\,\Psi\,\,}(\xi)\big|\lesssim 1,\\
&{\rm supp}\,\Psi^{\ast}\subset(-1,1),\quad\Psi^{\ast}\equiv 1\,\,{\rm for}\,\,v\in[-7/8,7/8],\quad\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\big|e^{\langle\xi\rangle^{3/4}}\widehat{\,\,\Psi^{\ast}}(\xi)\big|\lesssim 1.\end{split}

(2.38)

Set
\Psi_{L}(v):=\Psi(v/L),\Psi^{\ast}_{L}(v):=\Psi(v/L)
for
v\in\mathbb{R}
.
Assume that
\phi\in L^{2}(-2L,2L)
satisfies the elliptic equation for
v\in(-2L,2L)
,

(\alpha^{2}/L^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})\phi(v)+a(v)\phi(v)=h(v),

(2.39)

where non-homogenous term
h
satisfies

\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{\delta\langle L\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\big|\widehat{\big(\Psi_{L}^{\ast}h\big)}(\xi)\big|^{2}\,d\xi<\infty.

Then we have the following bounds

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha^{2}/L^{2}+\xi^{2})^{2}e^{2\delta\langle L\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\Big|\widehat{\big(\Psi_{L}\phi\big)}(\xi)\Big|^{2}\,d\xi\\
&\lesssim_{C_{2},M_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\delta\langle L\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\Big|\widehat{\big(\Psi^{\ast}_{L}h\big)}(\xi)\Big|^{2}\,d\xi+e^{-\alpha/200}\bigg[L^{-4}\int_{-2L}^{2L}|\phi(v)|^{2}dv+\int_{-2L}^{2L}|h(v)|^{2}\,dv\bigg].\end{split}

(2.40)

Remark 2.6
.

The main point of the estimate is that we can bound the solution using the same Gevrey norms as the nonhomogeneous term, without any loss of regularity. We remark also that generally the coefficients of the equation need to be more regular than the solutions.

Proof.

By a rescaling we can assume that
L=1
. The main idea is to represent
\phi
using the integral of Green’s function against the non-homogeneous term
h
which can be estimated precisely using regularity properties of the Green’s function, and a part which solves a homogeneous equation and enjoys better regularity property thanks to the smoothness of the coefficients of the equation.

For this purpose, we need to study the Green’s function for the differential operator
\alpha^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2}+a(v)
, which unfortunately may not be well defined over
(-2,2)
. To fix the issue, we choose for
c\in(0,1)
a smooth cutoff function
\Psi_{c}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
with
\Psi_{c}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(-c,c)
,
\Psi_{c}\equiv 1
on
[-7c/8,7c/8]
and
\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\big|e^{\langle\xi\rangle^{4/5}}\widehat{\,\Psi_{c}}(\xi)\big|\lesssim_{c}1.
Recall that
\alpha\in[1/100,\infty)
. We can find
c\in(0,1/4)
such that

\alpha^{2}/2-\partial_{v}^{2}-2\|a\|_{L^{\infty}(-2,\,2)}\mathbf{1}_{(-c,\,c)}(v)

is a positive operator on
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. The size of
c
depends only on
\|a\|_{L^{\infty}(-2,2)}
. Fix any
v_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
with
[v_{0}-c,v_{0}+c]\subseteq[-2,2]
. Define for
v\in\mathbb{R}
,

a_{c}(v):=a(v)\Psi_{c}(v-v_{0}).

(2.41)

Again for simplicity of notations we suppressed the dependence of
a_{c}
on
v_{0}
which is fixed.
Then we can define for the interval
I:=[v_{0}-c,c_{0}+c]
the Green’s function
g_{I}(v,\rho)
which satisfies

\begin{split}&\big(\alpha^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2}\big)g_{I}(v,\rho)+a_{c}(v)g_{I}(v,\rho)=\delta(v-\rho),\qquad{\rm for}\,\,v\in\mathbb{R},\,\rho\in I.\end{split}

(2.42)

Standard energy estimates imply that for
\rho\in I

|\alpha|\big\|g_{I}(v,\rho)\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\big\|\partial_{v}g_{I}(v,\rho)\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim 1.

(2.43)

Set for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

g_{I}^{\ast}(v,\rho):=g_{I}(v,\rho)\Psi_{c}(\rho-v_{0}).

(2.44)

We claim the following bounds.

Claim 2.7
.

For sufficiently small
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
depending only on
C_{2},M_{2}
, such that we have the bounds for all
\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}
,

\big|\widehat{\,\,g_{I}^{\ast}\,}(\xi,\eta)\big|\lesssim\frac{e^{-\delta^{\prime}\langle\xi+\eta\rangle^{1/2}}}{\alpha^{2}+\xi^{2}}.

(2.45)

Assume the claim for a moment. We decompose
h
on the interval
[v_{0}-7c/8,v_{0}+7c/8]
in the following way

\phi(v)=\phi_{1}(v)+\phi_{2}(v):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g^{\ast}_{I}(v,\rho)h(\rho)\,d\rho+\phi_{2}(v),

(2.46)

where
\phi_{2}
satisfies on
[v_{0}-3c/4,v_{0}+3c/4]

(\alpha^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})\phi_{2}(v)+a(v)\phi_{2}(v)=0.

(2.47)

The desired bounds (
2.40
) then follow from the bounds (
2.45
), proposition
2.4
, and a standard partition of unity argument, except for the exponential factor
e^{-\alpha/200}
. To see the exponential factor in (
2.40
), we note that it is important only for large
\alpha
. In that case the Green’s function
g
can be defined globally on
(-2,2)
and the desired bounds (
2.40
) follows from proposition (
2.4
).
∎

It remains to give

Proof of Claim
2.7
.

It follows from (
2.42
) that
g_{I}^{\ast}(v,\rho)
satisfies for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
the equation

\begin{split}&(\alpha^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2}+a_{c}(v))g_{I}^{\ast}(v,\rho)=\delta(v-\rho)\Psi_{c}(\rho-v_{0}).\end{split}

(2.48)

Define for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

h_{I}(v,\rho):=g_{I}^{\ast}(v+\rho,\rho),

(2.49)

then by (
2.48
) we have for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

\begin{split}&(\alpha^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2}+a_{c}(v+\rho))h_{I}(v,\rho)=\delta(v)\Psi_{c}(\rho-v_{0})\end{split}

(2.50)

It is clear from a change of variable that (
2.45
) follows from the inequality that for
\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}

\big|\widehat{\,\,h_{I}}(\xi,\eta)\big|\lesssim\frac{e^{-\delta^{\prime}\langle\eta\rangle^{1/2}}}{\alpha^{2}+\xi^{2}}.

(2.51)

We divide the proof of the bounds (
2.51
) into several steps.

Step 1: low frequency bounds.
(
2.43
) implies that

\big\|(|\alpha|+|\xi|)\widehat{\,h_{I}}(\xi,\eta)\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim 1.

(2.52)

Step 2: high frequency integral bounds.
In this step we use a commutator argument to prove

\big\|(|\alpha|+|\xi|)e^{\delta^{\prime}\langle\eta\rangle^{1/2}}\widehat{\,h_{I}}(\xi,\eta)\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim 1,

(2.53)

for a suitable
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
depending only on
C_{2},M_{2}
.
Define the Fourier multiplier operator
A
as follows. For any
\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,

\widehat{\,A\varphi\,}(\eta):=e^{\delta^{\prime}\langle\eta\rangle^{1/2}}\widehat{\varphi}(\eta),\quad{\rm for}\,\,\eta\in\mathbb{R}.

(2.54)

Applying
A
to (
2.48
) (acting on the variable
\rho
) we obtain

\begin{split}&(\alpha^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})A\big[h_{I}(v,\cdot)\big](\rho)+a_{c}(v+\rho)A\big[\,h_{I}(v,\cdot)\big](\rho)\\
&=\delta(v)A\big[\Psi(\cdot-v_{0})\big](\rho)+a_{c}(v+\rho)A\big[\,h_{I}(v,\cdot)\big](\rho)-A\big[a_{c}(v+\cdot)h_{I}(v,\cdot)\big](\rho).\end{split}

(2.55)

Standard energy estimates (by multiplying
A\big[h_{I}(v,\cdot)\big](\rho)
to (
2.55
) and integrating in
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
) and Sobolev inequality imply

\begin{split}&\left\|(|\alpha|+|\xi|)A(\eta)\widehat{\,h_{I}}(\xi,\eta)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\\
&\lesssim 1+\Big\|a_{c}(v+\rho)A\big[\,h_{I}(v,\cdot)\big](\rho)-A\big[a_{c}(v+\cdot)h_{I}(v,\cdot)\big](\rho)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.\end{split}

(2.56)

We have the following commutator estimates using (
2.52
), for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
and suitable
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
,

\begin{split}&\Big\|a_{c}(v+\rho)A\big[\,h_{I}(v,\cdot)\big](\rho)-A\big[a_{c}(v+\cdot)h_{I}(v,\cdot)\big](\rho)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\\
&\lesssim\Big\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{\,a_{c}}(\beta)\big[A(\eta-\beta)-A(\eta)\big]\widehat{\,h_{I}}(\xi-\beta,\eta-\beta)\,d\gamma\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\\
&\lesssim\Big\|\langle\eta\rangle^{-1/2}A(\eta)\widehat{\,h_{I}}(\xi,\eta)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim\gamma\big\|A\big[\,h_{I}(v,\cdot)\big](\rho)\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+C_{\gamma}.\end{split}

(2.57)

Combining (
2.52
), (
2.56
) and (
2.57
), we obtain the desired bounds (
2.53
).

Step 3: the pointwise bound.
The desired bounds (
2.51
) follow from (
2.53
) and equation (
2.48
).
∎

3.
Bounds on the Green’s function associated with a long range potential

In this section we define and study the property of a Green’s function associated with a long range potential which is important in proving the “vortex depletion” phenomenon. Define for
w\in(-\infty,-5]
the potential

V_{w}(v):=\frac{e^{2v}D(v)}{B(v)-B(w)}\Big[\mathbf{1}_{[w+2,\infty)}\ast\Psi^{\dagger}\Big](v),

(3.1)

where
\Psi^{\dagger}
is a nonnegative Gevrey regular cutoff function satisfying

{\rm supp}\,\Psi^{\dagger}\subseteq[-1,1],\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Psi^{\dagger}=1,\quad{\rm and}\quad\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|e^{\langle\xi\rangle^{4/5}}\widehat{\,\Psi\,}(\xi)\Big|\lesssim 1.

We note that
V_{w}\geq 0
on
\mathbb{R}
, which is important in applying the maximum principle below.
We begin with a simple result which will be useful for later applications.

Lemma 3.1
.

Assume that
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
and
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
with
A<A^{\prime}
. For
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
, suppose
g_{k}(\cdot,\rho)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
is the solution to

(k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})g_{k}(v,\rho)+8{\bf 1}_{[A,\,A^{\prime}]}(v)g_{k}(v,\rho)=\delta(v-\rho),\qquad{\rm for}\,\,v\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.2)

Set
m:=\min(\rho,v),M:=\max(\rho,v)
,
\mu_{k}:=\sqrt{k^{2}+8}
and
d:=|[m,M]\cap[A,\,A^{\prime}]|
, then we have the following bounds for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

|g_{k}(v,\rho)|\lesssim\frac{1}{|k|}e^{-|k||v-\rho|}e^{-(\mu_{k}-|k|)d}.

(3.3)

In the above, the implied constant is independent of
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\},A,A^{\prime},\rho
and
v\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

The proof follows from slightly complicated but explicit calculations. By translation symmetry, we can assume that
A<A^{\prime}=0
. We consider several cases, depending on the range of
\rho
. We can assume
k>0
, without loss of generality.

Case 1:
\rho\leq A
.
Direct calculations show that for
v\in\mathbb{R}
,

\begin{split}g_{k}(v,\rho)=&c_{1}e^{k(v-\rho)}{\bf 1}_{(-\infty,\rho]}(v)+\Big[c_{2}e^{k(v-\rho)}+c_{3}e^{-k(v-\rho)}\Big]{\bf 1}_{(\rho,A)}(v)\\
&+\Big[c_{4}e^{\mu_{k}v}+c_{5}e^{-\mu_{k}v}\Big]{\bf 1}_{[A,0]}(v)+c_{6}e^{-kv}{\bf 1}_{(0,\infty)}(v),\end{split}

(3.4)

where the coefficients
c_{j},j\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[1,6]
are determined by the requirement that
g_{k}(v,\rho)
is
C^{1}
except when
v=\rho
where the derivative in
v
has a jump of unit size. More precise, we have

\begin{split}&c_{1}=c_{2}+c_{3},\quad kc_{1}-kc_{2}+kc_{3}=1,c_{2}\,e^{k(A-\rho)}+c_{3}\,e^{-k(A-\rho)}=c_{4}\,e^{\mu_{k}A}+c_{5}\,e^{-\mu_{k}A},\\
&c_{2}k\,e^{k(A-\rho)}-c_{3}k\,e^{-k(A-\rho)}=c_{4}\mu_{k}\,e^{\mu_{k}A}-c_{5}\mu_{k}\,e^{-\mu_{k}A},\\
&c_{4}+c_{5}=c_{6},\quad c_{4}\mu_{k}-c_{5}\mu_{k}=-c_{6}k.\end{split}

(3.5)

Routine calculations then show that

\begin{split}&|c_{1}|\lesssim\frac{1}{2k},\quad c_{2}\approx-\frac{1}{k^{3}}e^{-2k(A-\rho)},\quad c_{3}=\frac{1}{2k},\quad c_{4}=\frac{\mu_{k}-k}{2\mu_{k}}c_{6}\\
&c_{5}=\frac{\mu_{k}+k}{2\mu_{k}}c_{6},\quad c_{6}\approx\frac{1}{k}e^{k\rho}e^{(\mu_{k}-k)A}.\end{split}

(3.6)

The desired bounds (
3.11
) follow from (
3.14
) and (
3.4
)-(
3.6
) in this case.

Case 2:
\rho\in[A,0]
.
We have by direct computation that for
v\in\mathbb{R}
,

\begin{split}g_{k}(v,\rho)=&c_{1}e^{k(v-A)}{\bf 1}_{(-\infty,A]}(v)+\Big[c_{2}e^{\mu_{k}(v-\rho)}+c_{3}e^{-\mu_{k}(v-\rho)}\Big]{\bf 1}_{(A,\rho)}(v)\\
&+\Big[c_{4}e^{\mu_{k}(v-\rho)}+c_{5}e^{-\mu_{k}(v-\rho)}\Big]{\bf 1}_{[\rho,0]}(v)+c_{6}e^{-kv}{\bf 1}_{(0,\infty)}(v),\end{split}

(3.7)

where similar to (
3.5
) we have

\begin{split}&c_{1}=c_{2}\,e^{\mu_{k}(A-\rho)}+c_{3}\,e^{-\mu_{k}(A-\rho)},\quad c_{1}k=c_{2}\mu_{k}\,e^{\mu_{k}(A-\rho)}-c_{3}\mu_{k}\,e^{-\mu_{k}(A-\rho)},\\
&c_{2}+c_{3}=c_{4}+c_{5},\quad c_{2}\mu_{k}-c_{3}\mu_{k}-(c_{4}\mu_{k}-c_{5}\mu_{k})=1,\\
&c_{4}e^{-\mu_{k}\rho}+c_{5}e^{\mu_{k}\rho}=c_{6},\quad c_{4}\mu_{k}e^{-\mu_{k}\rho}-c_{5}\mu_{k}e^{\mu_{k}\rho}=-c_{6}k.\end{split}

(3.8)

Routine calculations show

\begin{split}&c_{1}\approx e^{-2\mu_{k}\rho+\mu_{k}A}c_{6},\quad c_{2}=\frac{\mu_{k}+k}{2\mu_{k}}e^{-\mu_{k}(A-\rho)}c_{1}\approx\frac{1}{\mu_{k}},\quad c_{3}=\frac{\mu_{k}-k}{2\mu_{k}}e^{\mu_{k}(A-\rho)}c_{1},\\
&c_{4}=\frac{\mu_{k}-k}{2\mu_{k}}e^{\mu_{k}\rho}c_{6},\quad c_{5}=\frac{\mu_{k}+k}{2\mu_{k}}e^{-\mu_{k}\rho}c_{6}\approx\frac{1}{\mu_{k}},\quad c_{6}\approx\frac{1}{k}e^{\mu_{k}\rho}.\end{split}

(3.9)

The desired bounds (
3.11
) follow from (
3.14
) and (
3.7
)-(
3.9
) in this case.

Case 3:
\rho\geq 0
.
(
3.11
) can be proved using similar calculations as in Case 1, or using symmetry of the bounds.

∎

We are now ready to prove a key estimate on the Green’s function associated with the nonlocal potential
V_{w}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Assume that
w\leq-5
. For
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
, let
\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)
be the Green’s function to the differential operator
k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2}+V_{w}
on
\mathbb{R}
, i.e.,

(k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2}+V_{w})\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)=\delta(v-\rho),

(3.10)

for
v\in\mathbb{R},\rho\in\mathbb{R}
. Set
m:=\min(\rho,v),M:=\max(\rho,v)
,
\mu_{k}:=\sqrt{k^{2}+8}
and
d:=|[m,M]\cap[w,0]|
, then we have the following bounds

|\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)|\lesssim\frac{1}{|k|}e^{-|k||v-\rho|}e^{-(\mu_{k}-|k|)d}=\varpi_{k,w}(v,\rho)/|k|.

(3.11)

In addition, we have the following bounds for the derivatives for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

|\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)|\lesssim\varpi_{k,w}(v,\rho),

(3.12)

|\partial_{v\rho}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)-\delta(v-\rho)|\lesssim|k|\varpi_{k,w}(v,\rho).

(3.13)

Remark 3.3
.

The improved decay given by the additional factor
e^{-(\mu_{k}-|k|)d}
is at the heart of the vortex depletion phenomenon.

Proof.

We can assume that
k\geq 1
without loss of generality. Since
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
and
w\leq-5
are fixed throughout the proof, for the simplicity of notations, we suppress the dependence on
k,w
of various quantities, when there is no possibility of confusion.

Step 1: proof of bounds (
3.11
).
We can assume that
w\leq-5
satisfies
|w|\gg 1
, since otherwise the desired bounds (
3.11
) follow from the fact that
V_{w}\geq 0
on
\mathbb{R}
and the comparison principle. Fix
A>10
sufficiently large, to be determined later. Set for
w\leq-2A
,
V_{A}^{\ast}(v):=8\mathbf{1}_{[w+A,-A]}(v)
for
v\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
G^{\ast}_{A}(v,\rho)
be the Green’s function for
k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2}+V_{A}^{\ast}
on
\mathbb{R}
. Set also
V_{A}^{\ast\ast}(v):=\frac{e^{2v}D(v)}{B(v)-B(w)}\mathbf{1}_{[w+A,-A]}(v)
for
v\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
G^{\ast\ast}_{A}(v,\rho)
be the Green’s function for
k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2}+V_{A}^{\ast\ast}
on
\mathbb{R}
. Then by the comparison principle, for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)\leq G_{A}^{\ast\ast}(v,\rho).

(3.14)

To prove the desired bounds (
3.11
), it suffices to prove for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
and
w\leq-2A
,

G_{A}^{\ast\ast}(v,\rho)\lesssim_{A}\frac{1}{|k|}e^{-|k||v-\rho|}e^{-(\mu_{k}-|k|)d}.

(3.15)

The main idea to prove (
3.15
) is to compare
G_{A}^{\ast\ast}
and
G_{A}^{\ast}
. Writing for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

G_{A}^{\ast\ast}(v,\rho):=G_{A}^{\ast}(v,\rho)+g_{A}(v,\rho).

(3.16)

Then
g_{A}
satisfies for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

\big(k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2}+V_{A}^{\ast}(v)\big)g_{A}(v,\rho)=\big(V_{A}^{\ast}(v)-V_{A}^{\ast\ast}(v)\big)g_{A}(v,\rho)+\big(V_{A}^{\ast}(v)-V_{A}^{\ast\ast}(v)\big)G_{A}^{\ast}(v,\rho).

(3.17)

We use the bounds for
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
, which follows from simple calculations in view of (
1.35
)-(
1.36
),

\big|V_{A}^{\ast}(\rho)-V_{A}^{\ast\ast}(\rho)\big|\lesssim e^{-A/2}\big[e^{-|\rho-w|}+e^{-|\rho|}\big].

(3.18)

Denote for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
and
L_{A}:=\big|\big[\min\{\rho,v\},\max\{v,\rho\}\big]\cap\big[w+A,-A\big]\big|
, the weight function

\zeta_{A}(v,\rho):=e^{k|v-\rho|}e^{(\mu_{k}-k)L_{A}}.

(3.19)

Then for
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, it is easy to check that

\zeta_{A}(v,\rho)\leq\zeta_{A}(v,\sigma)\zeta_{A}(\sigma,\rho).

(3.20)

Let

\alpha:=\sup_{v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}}\big[\zeta_{A}(v,\rho)|g_{A}(v,\rho)|\Big],

(3.21)

which is finite since
|g_{A}(v,\rho)|\lesssim(1/k)e^{-k|v-\rho|}
.
By Lemma
3.1
, we have for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

k\zeta_{A}(v,\rho)G_{A}^{\ast}(v,\rho)\lesssim 1.

(3.22)

Using (
3.17
), (
3.18
) and (
3.22
) we can bound

\begin{split}&k\zeta_{A}(v,\rho)|g_{A}(v,\rho)|\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}}k\zeta_{A}(v,\rho)G_{A}^{\ast}(v,\sigma)e^{-A/2}\big[e^{-|\sigma-w|/2}+e^{-|\sigma|/2}\big]\big(|g_{A}(\sigma,\rho)|+G_{A}^{\ast}(\sigma,\rho)\big)\,d\sigma\\
&\lesssim e^{-A/2}\alpha+e^{-A/2},\end{split}

(3.23)

which implies that

\alpha\lesssim e^{-A/2}\alpha+e^{-A/2}.

(3.24)

Choosing
A
large we obtain the desired bounds (
3.15
) from (
3.16
), (
3.22
) and (
3.24
).

Step 2: proof of the bounds (
3.12
).

We now turn to the proof of (
3.12
). For
w\leq-10
,
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
, we note that the quantity

\frac{1}{k}e^{-k|v-\rho|}e^{-(\mu_{k}-k)d}

varies at most by
O(1)
over an interval of
v
of size
O(1/k)
. Denote for fixed
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

I_{v}:=[v-2/k,v+2/k]\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\gamma:=\frac{1}{k}e^{-k|v-\rho|}e^{-(\mu_{k}-k)d}.

Fix a smooth cutoff function
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(I_{v})
such that
\varphi\equiv 1
on
[v-1/k,v+1/k]
and
|\partial_{v^{\prime}}\varphi(v^{\prime})|\leq 2k
for all
v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
. We notice the bounds

\sup_{v^{\prime}\in I_{v}}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v^{\prime},\rho)\lesssim\gamma.

(3.25)

Using the equation (
3.10
) and the bounds (
3.11
) we obtain for some
C_{0}\in(0,\infty)
that

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}}k^{2}|\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v^{\prime},\rho)|^{2}\varphi^{2}(v^{\prime})+|\partial_{v^{\prime}}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v^{\prime},\rho)|^{2}\varphi^{2}(v^{\prime})\,dv^{\prime}\\
&\lesssim 2\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{v^{\prime}}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v^{\prime},\rho)||\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v^{\prime},\rho)||\varphi(v^{\prime})\partial_{v^{\prime}}\varphi(v^{\prime})|\,dv^{\prime}+\varphi^{2}(\rho)/k\\
&\leq C_{0}|k|\gamma^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{v^{\prime}}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v^{\prime},\rho)|\varphi^{2}(v^{\prime})\,dv^{\prime},\end{split}

(3.26)

which implies that

\int_{v-1/k}^{v+1/k}\big|\partial_{v^{\prime}}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v^{\prime},\rho)\big|^{2}\,dv^{\prime}\lesssim k\gamma^{2}.

(3.27)

Therefore there exists
v^{\prime}\in[v-1/|k|,v+1/|k|]
such that

|\partial_{v^{\prime}}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v^{\prime},\rho)|\lesssim|k|\gamma.

(3.28)

The equation (
3.10
) and the bounds (
3.11
) imply that

\sup_{v^{\prime\prime}\in I_{v},v^{\prime\prime}\neq\rho}|\partial^{2}_{v^{\prime\prime}}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v^{\prime\prime},\rho)|\lesssim k^{2}\gamma.

(3.29)

The desired bounds (
3.12
) follow from (
3.28
)-(
3.29
), together with the fact that at
v^{\prime\prime}=\rho
the jump in the derivative of
\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)
is of unit size.

Step 3: proof of (
3.13
).
Recall that for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

(k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)+V_{w}(v)\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)=\delta(v-\rho).

(3.30)

Thus for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

\begin{split}&(k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)+V_{w}(v)\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)+\partial_{v}V_{w}(v)\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)=\partial_{v}\delta(v-\rho),\\
&(k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})\partial_{\rho}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)+V_{w}(v)\partial_{\rho}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)=-\partial_{v}\delta(v-\rho).\end{split}

(3.31)

If we write

\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)=-\partial_{\rho}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)+\mathcal{H}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho),

(3.32)

then

(k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})\mathcal{H}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)+V_{w}(v)\mathcal{H}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)=-\partial_{v}V_{w}(v)\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho).

(3.33)

Therefore

\mathcal{H}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\sigma)\partial_{\sigma}V_{w}(\sigma)\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(\sigma,\rho)\,d\sigma,

(3.34)

and

\big|\partial_{v}\mathcal{H}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)\big|=\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{v}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\sigma)\partial_{\sigma}V_{w}(\sigma)\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(\sigma,\rho)\,d\sigma\Big|\lesssim k^{-1}e^{-k|v-\rho|-(\mu_{k}-k)d}.

(3.35)

The desired bound (
3.13
) follows from (
3.30
), (
3.32
) and (
3.35
).

∎

For later applications, we prove the following refined property for the Green’s function
\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v,\rho)
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Assume that
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
and
\mu_{k}:=\sqrt{k^{2}+8}
. Fix
\Psi_{1}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(-8,8)
with
\Psi_{1}\equiv 1
on
[-1,1]
and
\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\big|e^{\langle\xi\rangle^{7/8}}\widehat{\,\,\Psi_{1}\,\,}(\xi)\big|\lesssim 1
. Let
w_{\ast}\in(-\infty,-15]
. Define for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
and
w\leq-5
,

\mathcal{F}_{k}(v,\rho,w):=\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v+w,\rho+w),

(3.36)

and for notational convenience (for this proposition only) define also for
v,\rho,w\in\mathbb{R}
,

\qquad Q_{k}^{w_{\ast}}(v,\rho,w):=\mathcal{F}_{k}(v,\rho,w)\Psi_{1}(w-w_{\ast}).

(3.37)

Let

\widehat{\,\,Q_{k}^{w_{\ast}}}(v,\rho,\xi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}Q_{k}^{w_{\ast}}(v,\rho,w)e^{-iw\xi}\,dw,\qquad{\rm for}\,\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.38)

Then we have the following bounds for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
,
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\Big[e^{2\delta_{0}\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\big|\widehat{\,\,Q_{k}^{w_{\ast}}}(v,\rho,\xi)\big|\Big]\lesssim|k|^{-1}\varpi_{\varkappa_{k},w_{\ast}}(v+w_{\ast},\rho+w_{\ast})

(3.39)

In addition, we also have the bounds on the derivatives for
v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,

\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\Big[e^{2\delta_{0}\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\big|\partial_{v}\widehat{\,\,Q_{k}^{w_{\ast}}}(v,\rho,\xi)\big|\Big]\lesssim\varpi_{\varkappa_{k},w_{\ast}}(v+w_{\ast},\rho+w_{\ast}).

(3.40)

and

\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\Big[e^{2\delta_{0}\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\Big|\partial_{v\rho}\widehat{\,\,Q_{k}^{w_{\ast}}}(v,\rho,\xi)-\delta(v-\rho)\widehat{\,\,\Psi_{1}}(\xi)\Big|\Big]\lesssim|k|\varpi_{\varkappa_{k},w_{\ast}}(v+w_{\ast},\rho+w_{\ast}).

(3.41)

Proof.

The main idea is to use the pointwise bounds in lemma
3.2
and the commutator argument. Since the parameter
w_{\ast}
is fixed throughout the proof, for the simplicity of notations, we set
Q_{k}(v,\rho,w):=Q_{k}^{w_{\ast}}(v,\rho,w)
. By (
3.11
) we have

\big\|Q_{k}(v,\rho,w)\big\|_{L^{2}(w\in\mathbb{R})}\lesssim|k|^{-1}\varpi_{\varkappa_{k},w_{\ast}}(v+w_{\ast},\rho+w_{\ast}).

(3.42)

Let
A
be the Fourier multiplier operator such that for any
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,

\widehat{\,\,Ah\,\,}(\xi):=e^{2\delta_{0}\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\widehat{\,\,h\,\,}(\xi),\qquad{\rm for}\,\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.43)

It follows from the definition (
3.11
) that for
v,\rho,w\in\mathbb{R}
,

(k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})Q_{k}(v,\rho,w)+V_{w}(v+w)Q_{k}(v,\rho,w)=\delta(v-\rho)\Psi_{1}(w-w_{\ast}).

(3.44)

Applying the operator
A
(in the variable
w
) to (
3.45
), we obtain that

\begin{split}&(k^{2}-\partial_{v}^{2})A\big[Q_{k}(v,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)+V_{w}(v+w)A\big[Q_{k}(v,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)\\
&=\delta(v-\rho)A\big[\Psi_{1}(\cdot-w_{\ast})\big](w)+V_{w}(v+w)A\big[Q_{k}(v,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)-A\big[V_{\cdot}(v+\cdot)Q_{k}(v,\rho,\cdot)\big](w).\end{split}

(3.45)

We can reformulate (
3.45
) in the integral form

\begin{split}&A\big[Q_{k}(v,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)=\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v+w,\rho+w)A\big[\Psi_{1}(\cdot-w_{\ast})\big](w)\\
&+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v+w,\sigma+w)\bigg\{V_{w}(\sigma+w)A\big[Q_{k}(\sigma,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)-A\big[V_{\cdot}(\sigma+\cdot)Q_{k}(\sigma,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)\bigg\}\,d\sigma.\end{split}

(3.46)

Denote

M:=|k|\sup_{v,\rho\in\mathbb{R}}\Big[\left\|A\big[Q_{k}(v,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)\right\|_{L^{2}(w\in\mathbb{R})}/(\varpi_{k,w_{\ast}}(v+w_{\ast},\rho+w_{\ast}))\Big].

(3.47)

Fix smooth cutoff functions
\Psi_{2}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(-17/2,17/2)
with
\Psi_{2}\equiv 1
on
[-33/4,33/4]
and

\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\big|e^{\langle\xi\rangle^{7/8}}\widehat{\Psi_{2}}(\xi)\big|\lesssim 1,

and
\Psi_{3}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(-9,9)
with
\Psi_{3}\equiv[-35/4,35/4]
and
\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\big|e^{\langle\xi\rangle^{7/8}}\widehat{\,\Psi_{3}}(\xi)\big|\lesssim 1
. We define

V_{\sigma,w_{\ast}}(w):=V_{w}(\sigma+w)\Psi_{3}(w-w_{\ast}),\qquad{\rm if}\,\,\sigma\in\mathbb{R}\backslash[-1,-w_{\ast}+1]

(3.48)

and

V_{\sigma,w_{\ast}}(w):=\big[V_{w}(\sigma+w)-8\big]\Psi_{3}(w-w_{\ast}),\qquad{\rm if}\,\,\sigma\in[-1,-w_{\ast}+1].

(3.49)

We have the bound (which follows from simple calculations in view of (
1.35
)-(
1.36
)) for
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
,

\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\big|e^{3\delta_{0}\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\widehat{\,V_{\sigma,w_{\ast}}}(\xi)\big|\lesssim e^{-|\sigma|}+e^{-|\sigma+w_{\ast}|}.

(3.50)

We observe that for
\sigma,\rho,w\in\mathbb{R}
,

\begin{split}&\Psi_{2}(w-w_{\ast})V_{w}(\sigma+w)A\big[Q_{k}(\sigma,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)-\Psi_{2}(w-w_{\ast})A\big[V_{\cdot}(\sigma+\cdot)Q_{k}(\sigma,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)\\
&=\Psi_{2}(w-w_{\ast})V_{\sigma,w_{\ast}}(w)A\big[Q_{k}(\sigma,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)-\Psi_{2}(w-w_{\ast})A\big[V_{\sigma,w_{\ast}}(\cdot)Q_{k}(\sigma,\rho,\cdot)\big](w).\end{split}

(3.51)

It follows from (
3.50
)-(
3.51
) and the bound (
3.42
) that for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
and suitable
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
,

\begin{split}&\left\|\Psi_{2}(w-w_{\ast})V_{w}(\sigma+w)A\big[Q_{k}(\sigma,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)-\Psi_{2}(w-w_{\ast})A\big[V_{\cdot}(\sigma+\cdot)Q_{k}(\sigma,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)\right\|_{L^{2}(w\in\mathbb{R})}^{2}\\
&\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big|\widehat{\,\,\,V_{\sigma,w_{\ast}}}(\alpha)\big|\big|\widehat{\,\,\Psi_{2}}(\beta)\big|\big|A(\xi-\alpha-\beta)-A(\xi-\beta)\big|\big|\widehat{\,\,Q_{k}}(\sigma,\rho,\xi-\alpha-\beta)\big|\,d\alpha d\beta\bigg]^{2}d\xi\\
&\lesssim\big[e^{-|\sigma|}+e^{-|\sigma+w_{\ast}|}\big]\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-\delta_{0}\langle\alpha\rangle^{1/2}-\langle\beta\rangle^{4/5}}\langle\xi-\alpha-\beta\rangle^{-1/2}\big|\widehat{\,\,Q_{k}}(v,\rho,\xi-\alpha-\beta)\big|\,d\alpha d\beta\bigg]^{2}d\xi\\
&\lesssim\big[e^{-|\sigma|}+e^{-|\sigma+w_{\ast}|}\big]\Big[C_{\gamma}\left\|Q_{k}(\sigma,\rho,w)\right\|_{L^{2}(w\in\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\gamma\left\|A\big[Q_{k}(\sigma,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)\right\|_{L^{2}(w\in\mathbb{R})}^{2}\Big]\\
&\lesssim\big[e^{-|\sigma|}+e^{-|\sigma+w_{\ast}|}\big]\Big[C_{\gamma}\big(\varpi_{\varkappa_{k},w_{\ast}}(\sigma+w_{\ast},\rho+w_{\ast})/|k|\big)^{2}+\gamma\big(M\varpi_{\varkappa_{k},w_{\ast}}(\sigma+w_{\ast},\rho+w_{\ast})/|k|\big)^{2}\Big].\end{split}

(3.52)

In the above, the parameter
\gamma
is small and is used to divide the frequency to the cases
|\xi|>\gamma^{-1}
and
|\xi|\leq\gamma^{-1}
, to obtain the crucial gain of the factor
\gamma
. It follows from (
3.52
) that

\begin{split}&\Big\|\Psi_{2}(w-w_{\ast})\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{G}_{k}^{w}(v+w,\sigma+w)\bigg\{V_{w}(\sigma+w)A\big[Q_{k}(\sigma,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)\\
&\hskip 166.2212pt-A\big[V_{w}(\sigma+\cdot)Q_{k}(\sigma,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)\bigg\}\,d\sigma\Big\|_{L^{2}(w\in\mathbb{R})}\\
&\lesssim C_{\gamma}\varpi_{\varkappa_{k},w_{\ast}}(v+w_{\ast},\rho+w_{\ast})/|k|+\gamma^{1/2}M\varpi_{\varkappa_{k},w_{\ast}}(v+w_{\ast},\rho+w_{\ast})/|k|.\end{split}

(3.53)

We also have for all
\gamma\in(0,1)
and suitable
C_{\gamma}\in(0,\infty)
,

\begin{split}&\left\|A\big[Q_{k}(v,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)-\Psi_{2}(w-w_{\ast})A\big[Q_{k}(v,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)\right\|_{L^{2}(w\in\mathbb{R})}^{2}\\
&=\left\|A\big[\Psi_{2}(\cdot-w_{\ast})Q_{k}(v,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)-\Psi_{2}(w-w_{\ast})A\big[Q_{k}(v,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)\right\|_{L^{2}(w\in\mathbb{R})}^{2}\\
&\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|\widehat{\,\,\Psi_{2}}(\alpha)\big|\big|A(\xi-\alpha)-A(\xi)\big|\big|\widehat{\,\,Q_{k}}(\sigma,\rho,\xi-\alpha)\big|\,d\alpha\bigg]^{2}\,d\xi\\
&\lesssim C_{\gamma}\big(\varpi_{\varkappa_{k},w_{\ast}}(v+w_{\ast},\rho+w_{\ast})/|k|\big)^{2}+\gamma\left\|A\big[Q_{k}(v,\rho,\cdot)\big](w)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(w\in\mathbb{R})},\end{split}

(3.54)

which together with (
3.53
), upon choosing
\gamma\in(0,1)
sufficiently small, implies that
